About the Author
INTRODUCTION

The physical world is a large and wonderful place, but it is also
confusing, and there is much about it that no one quite understands.
There are also many phenomena that some of us understand pre y
well, but others do not.

One of the reasons that most of us don't know as much about the
world as we might is simply that we don't bother to think about it.
Which is not to say that we don't think at all. Everyone thinks, but
each person tends to concentrate mostly on ma ers that seem to be
of immediate importance. What shall we have for dinner? How do I
pay my bills? Where shall I go for a vaca on? How can I go about
ge ng a promo on and a raise in pay? Shall I try to arrange a date
with so-and-so? What's this funny pain I have in my side?

These are such important ques ons to each of us, and our need to
answer them is o en so strong that there is simply no me to wonder
about more general issues such as: What is the shape of the Earth? A
natural response to a ques on like this might be: "Who cares? Why do
you bother me with such silly things? What difference does it make?"

But it does make a difference. For example, you can't sail a ship
across the ocean and reach your des na on by the shortest possible
route, or fire a missile and expect it to land on its target, without
knowing the shape of the Earth.

But aside from that, and far more important, is that wondering
about such ques ons is fascina ng, and finding the answers is fairly
easy if you're systema c about it. The aim of this book is to bring these
general ques ons closer to home, by exploring their answers in terms
that anyone can follow, making the complexi es of the universe
absolutely clear.

Of course, one ques on usually leads to another. Knowledge about
the world is not a straight line but an intricately connected three-
dimensional lacework, so that answering a par cular ques on
some mes requires an explana on of something else, which in turn
demands the explana on of s ll another thing, and so on. I will,
however, a empt to unravel the threads with as much care as possible
so that not too much has to be explained at any one me.
Nevertheless, it might s ll be necessary for me to jump about a bit
now and then, and I ask forgiveness for that.

Then, too, as we advance from ques on to ques on, simple
reasoning in some cases will not be enough; we will have to know a
li le about what scien sts have observed and deduced. But I will try to
describe that work with par cular care and, wherever possible,
without complex mathema cs or diagrams. Thinking always leads to
more thinking, and there's no end to it. To people who enjoy thinking,
that is the glory of science. People who don't enjoy thinking about
things that don't concern them immediately, find the necessity of
con nuing to do so indefinitely frightening, and they turn away from
science. I hope you are in the first group.

So let us get started with the ques on I have already asked and see
where that will take us.
1. WHAT IS THE SHAPE OF THE EARTH?

To begin with, we must look around us and see that the Earth is
uneven and has no easily described shape. Even if we ignore houses
and other man-made objects, and all living things as well, we are s ll
le with an uneven surface of bare rock and soil.

The first conclusion we would come to, then, would be that the
Earth is a lumpy object with hills and valleys, cliffs and ravines. In
places like Colorado, Peru, or Nepal, where there are towering
mountains that reach miles into the air, the Earth's irregularity is very
clear. But if you live in some parts of Kansas or Uruguay or the Ukraine,
you don't see much in the way of hills or valleys; you see plains, which
look pre y flat.

Then, too, even if you do encounter hills and mountains, the Earth
may rise on one side, but then fall again on the other side. Valleys and
ravines may slant down on one side, but slope up on the other. No
part of the Earth's land surface goes up without ever coming down
again as you move across it; no part goes down without ever going
back up. It seems reasonable, then, to conclude that the Earth is, on
the average, flat.

Again, if you were to paddle a boat out onto a body of water so
large that you couldn't see land in any direc on, you would have only
the surface of the water to consider. This surface is uneven because it
is full of waves. Yet if there is no wind, the waves are not large, and it
is easy to see that, on the average, the water surface is flat. In fact,
water is much more nearly flat at all mes than land.

So it makes sense to suppose that the Earth is flat, and for
thousands of years that is exactly what human beings believed. Since a
flat Earth made sense and since it didn't take much thinking to see
that it made sense, why would anyone waste any further me thinking
about it?

Have you ever stood on a hilltop and looked out on the valley
below? The valley looks pre y flat and you can look farther and
farther outward, past houses, trees, rivers, and other faraway objects,
though the more distant they appear to be, the less detail you can
make out. What's more, the air isn't usually absolutely clear; bits of
fog and smoke obscure the very distant por on, which becomes a kind
of bluish haze where the Earth and sky seem to meet.

The mee ng place of earth and sky is called the horizon, from a
Greek word for boundary. If you're looking at a flat sec on of the
Earth, the horizon runs evenly from right to le , and such a line is
therefore called horizontal.

Suppose, however, that you look in another direc on at another hill
close by. You can't see past the top of the hill to the other side
because you can't see around curves. Therefore as you look at the top
of the hill, you see only the sky above it, and not the Earth sloping
downward beyond it. There is a sharp line that seems quite close to
you that marks off the hill against the sky. Thus, if you are looking out
over a stretch of land and see a distant misty horizon, you know you
are looking over some pre y flat territory, but if you see a sharp
nearby horizon, you are looking at a hilltop.

Imagine that you are out on the ocean on the deck of a ship. It is a
clear, bright, sunny day, and the sea is calm. The sea air is usually less
dusty and misty than land air, so you look off into the distance, and
there is the horizon--sharp. The sea meets the sky in a clear horizontal
line. You are clearly looking at a hilltop.

How can that be? There are no hills in the ocean, just flat water. The
only answer is that the ocean is not flat, but curved, and from your
height on the ship deck, you can see out only so far ll your eyesight
meets the top of the curve, and you can't see beyond it. If you go up
to a higher deck, you can see farther out before the curve cuts off your
view, and if you go to a lower deck, you can see less far out. What's
more, if you stand in one place and look all around you, you will see
that same sharp horizon at the same distance in every direc on; not
only does the ocean surface curve, but it curves in the same way and
to the same extent in every direc on--at least as nearly as one can
make out with the eyes.

But why should the ocean curve? It must be following the surface of
the Earth, and the Earth itself must be curving in all direc ons, too.
The curving is more obscure on the land because the land is more
uneven than the sea, and the air over the land is usually mis er.

Given that the Earth does curve, what kind of curve is it? If the Earth
curves in the same way in all direc ons, it must be a sphere, for that is
the only known surface that curves downward equally in all direc ons.
So just by looking and thinking we can see that the Earth is a sphere.
You might ask why people didn't study the horizons and come to
this conclusion thousands of years ago, but the trouble is, few people
thought about it at all. It was much simpler to think of the Earth as
flat, and flatness didn't raise any par cular problems in ancient mes.
A spherical Earth, as we shall soon see, does raise problems that
require further thought.

You might ask: Can we trust our eyes? Is looking at the horizon
enough? Actually, in this case it is, though we are frequently misled by
our eyes, if we don't examine the evidence carefully.

For instance, suppose you are at sea and can iden fy a ship in the
distance sailing toward the horizon. You watch it, and as it approaches
the horizon, you don't see the lower decks anymore; then, a er a
while, you don't see the upper decks, either. All you see are the
smokestacks (or the sails, if it is a sailing vessel), and then they
disappear, too. It's not just a ma er of distance, for if you had a
spyglass and watched through that, the ship would seem much larger
and closer, but you would s ll see it disappear first at the bo om, then
higher up, then s ll higher. What you are seeing is the ship sailing over
the top of the Earth's curve and down the other side.

The first person we know of who ever maintained that the Earth
was a sphere was the Greek philosopher Pythagoras (c. 580?c. 500
B.C.), who came up with this hypothesis in about 500 B.C.

There are other pieces of evidence that show the Earth to be a
sphere. Certain stars are visible from some points on Earth and not
from others, and during an eclipse of the moon a shadow of the Earth
falls on the moon that is always curved like the edge of a sphere. The
Greek philosopher Aristotle (384?322 B.C.) listed all the evidence for
the Earth's sphericity in about 340 B.C., and though it wasn't commonly
accepted at the me, no educated man has doubted it since. In the
Space Age of today, photographs have been taken of Earth from outer
space in which we can actually see that it is a sphere.
2. WHAT IS THE SIZE OF THE EARTH?

As long as people thought the Earth was flat, there was not much
reason to worry about how large it was. It might stretch on forever, for
all anyone knew, but forever is a hard concept to imagine. It was a lot
easier to think that the Earth had a definite size and that there was
some end to it somewhere. Even today, people speak of "traveling to
the ends of the Earth," though nowadays that is only a colorful phrase
and not meant to be taken literally.

Of course, the thought of an end to the Earth creates problems.
Suppose you traveled a long distance and finally reached the end.
Could you fall off? If the ocean stretched out to the end, might it pour
off un l it was all gone? People who bothered to think of such ma ers
had to work out a way of keeping that from happening. Perhaps the
world is rimmed by a solid bank of tall mountains, so that it looks like a
frying pan and nothing on the surface can fall off. Or perhaps the sky is
a piece of solid ma er that is curved like a fla ened hemisphere
(which is what it looks like) and it comes down to meet the Earth on all
sides, so that the Earth is a flat plate with a lid on it--that, too, would
keep things in place. Either solu on seemed sa sfactory.

You might s ll ask how large the flat world was. In very ancient
mes, when people could move about only on foot and didn't travel
much, it was assumed that the world was quite small and that only
one's own region existed. That is why when, in 2800 B.C., there was a
tremendous river flood in the Tigris-Euphrates river valley, the
Sumerians who lived there thought the en re world was covered, and
that na?ve no on has come down to us as the biblical tale of Noah's
flood.

As people learned to trade, however, and sent armies hither and
yon, and took to riding horses, the horizon of the world expanded, and
by 500 B.C., the Persian Empire stretched out from east to west over a
distance of 4,800 kilometers (3,000 miles).* West of that empire were
Greece, Italy, and other lands, and there was no sign of an end.

When the Greek philosophers realized that the Earth was a sphere,
however, they knew it had to have a definite size, and you couldn't get
away with just saying that it was "very large" or that it went on and on
"indefinitely." What's more, the size of the sphere could be judged
without necessarily going very far from home.

You see, whereas a flat Earth can stretch out indefinitely, a spherical
Earth curves, and the curve must come back on itself. To determine
the Earth's size, all you have to do is measure how much it curves; the
more sharply it curves, the smaller the sphere, and the more gently it
curves, the larger the sphere.

One thing we can be sure of is that the curve is very gentle, so that
the sphere is very large. We know this must be so simply because it
took so long to decide the Earth was spherical. If the sphere was small,
the curvature would be so sharp that it would be impossible not to
no ce it. The gentler the curvature, the fla er a small region of the

Earth would seem.

But how do we measure the extent of the curvature of the Earth?

Here's one way. Take a thin strip of metal and force it down on an

absolutely level stretch of the Earth, so that it touches the Earth at all

points. It will then be forced to follow the curve of the Earth. You can

then li that strip of metal and sight along it and see how much it has

curved downward. If the strip of metal is 1 kilometer long, then its

downward curve should be about 12? cen meters (5 inches--for 1

cen  meter,  which  is  equal  to  1/         of  a  kilometer,  is  equal  to  about
100,000

2/5 of an inch).

The trouble with taking this measurement is that it would be hard

to find a kilometer of Earth's land surface that was absolutely even and

to make a strip of metal that followed the curve exactly, so you would

end up with a figure you could by no means trust. Even a small error in

the shape of the metal strip would cause a big error in calcula ng the

size of the Earth. In other words, some experiments that work

perfectly well in theory hardly work at all in prac cal reality, and this

would be one of them. We have to look for something else.

Suppose, then, you have a long, straight rod that is pounded into

the Earth partway, so that it stands perfectly erect. If it is a clear, sunny

day, and the sun is directly overhead, the rod casts no shadow because

the sunlight is coming down about it on every side. But suppose

another rod is pounded into the Earth at an angle to the ver cal. Now

the sunlight strikes the rod and casts a shadow. If there are a series of

rods all s cking out of the ground for six feet, but at various angles,

each will cast a shadow of a different length. The greater the angle of

pping, the longer the shadow.
Thus, if we measure the lengths of the shadows compared with the
length of the rods, we can calculate the angle of pping without
actually measuring the angle. The branch of mathema cs that makes
this possible is called trigonometry, and it was worked out by the
ancient Greek mathema cians quite early on. The Greek philosopher
Thales (c. 624?c. 546 B.C.) is supposed to have used trigonometry as
early as 580 B.C. to measure the height of Egyp an pyramids from the
lengths of the shadows they cast.

But you don't have to p the rods yourself. Suppose you have a rod
perfectly erect in one place and another rod perfectly erect in another
place hundreds of miles away. In between those two places, the Earth
curves, so that if you consider one rod erect, the other is at an angle to
it, the size of the angle depending on the amount of curvature of the
Earth's surface.

About 240 B.C., the Greek philosopher Eratosthenes (c. 276?c. 196
B.C.) tried to make this very observa on. He was told that at the
Egyp an city of Syene on June 21, the sun was directly overhead at
noon, so that an erect rod cast no shadow. On that same day, in the
Egyp an city of Alexandria, where Eratosthenes lived, an erect rod did
cast a small shadow.

Eratosthenes measured the length of the shadow and compared it
with the length of the rod, and from that he was able to tell how far
the curvature of the Earth lted the rod in Alexandria compared with
the rod in Syene. He knew the distance from Syene to Alexandria, so if
there was that much curvature in that distance, he could figure out
how far the curvature would have to extend before it curved back on
itself and completed the sphere. He announced that the Earth's sphere
was, to use round numbers in our modern measurements, 40,000
kilometers (25,000 miles) around at the Equator, which is its
circumference, and 12,800 kilometers (8,000 miles) from side to side,
which is its diameter.

He was quite correct, and, remarkably, the discovery was achieved
twenty-two centuries ago, without Eratosthenes ever leaving home,
just by clever thinking and a simple measurement.

This doesn't mean, by the way, that Eratosthenes's work was fully
accepted. Others made similar measurements and got smaller results,
and even as late as the me of Christopher Columbus (1451?1506),
the general feeling was that the Earth's circumference was only about
29,000 kilometers (18,000 miles long), less than three fourths of the
true circumference. Columbus headed westward in 1492 because he
thought that Asia was only 4,800 kilometers (3,000 miles) away.
Actually, it was 16,000 kilometers (10,000 miles away), and if the
American con nents hadn't existed and he hadn't been able to end his
journey there, he would never have been heard from again.

The ma er was not finally se led ll 1522, when an expedi on
begun by the Portuguese explorer Ferdinand Magellan (c. 1480?1521)
became the first to sail completely around the world. Magellan didn't
make it to the end, for he was killed en route in the Philippine Islands,
but one ship with eighteen men aboard did make it, and that voyage
proved that Eratosthenes' measurement was correct.

*I shall use the metric system in this book and give distances in
kilometers, but I will place American units in parentheses. One
kilometer is equal to just about five eighths of a mile. The metric
system is used over the en re world except the United States.
3. IF THE EARTH IS A SPHERE, WHY DON'T WE SLIDE
OFF?

When children are first told that the Earth is a sphere, it seems to
puzzle them. The people on the other side of the Earth (in Australia,
for instance, if you are living in the United States) must be walking
about with their heads down and their feet up, so why don't they fall
off the Earth altogether? A er all, if you tried to walk on the ceiling,
you would fall off.

It's worse than that, actually. Suppose that you were living at the
very top of a spherical Earth (as you certainly seem to be, since the
Earth curves gently downward in every direc on). In that case, you
might be safe only as long as you stayed exactly where you were. If
you started moving away in any direc on, you would begin to slip
down an incline. The farther you went, the steeper the incline un l
you started sliding faster and faster and more and more helplessly and
eventually fell off the Earth altogether. If this were true, all the oceans
would have long since tumbled off the Earth, and all the air, too. In
other words, we come to the seemingly reasonable conclusion that it
is impossible to live on a spherical Earth and therefore that the Earth
cannot be a sphere.

But since the Earth is a sphere, there must be some mistake in our
thinking, and it arises from what we mean when we say down. If we
are standing erect and we want to indicate the direc on down, we
point to our feet. When we do so, we are also poin ng to the center of
the Earth, which is about 6,350 kilometers (3,950 miles) below our
feet. Assuming that down always means the center of the Earth, then
wherever you are on the Earth's surface, when you stand erect, the
soles of your feet are always facing in that direc on. Those Australians,
who are also standing erect, have the soles of their feet facing the
center of the Earth, too, and to them down seems to be in the
direc on of their feet, just as it does to us.

We are pulled downward, as is everything heavy, which means that
we are pulled toward the center of the Earth, as is everything else on
the planet's surface, regardless of where it is located. Since there is no
sensa on that the Earth is curving when we travel, and since its
surface seems more or less horizontal, and since down is always in the
direc on of our feet when we are standing erect, the Earth seems flat,
and nothing ever falls off it, which is another reason it took so long to
find out it was spherical. The first person to make it plain that
everything on Earth is a racted to its center was Aristotle, and the
force responsible for this a rac on is called gravity, from a La n word
meaning heavy.

Suppose you have a huge quan ty of ma er of any shape, and
every part of it is pulled by every other part so that all the material is
packed as close as possible. When all the parts are compressed as
much as possible and can get no closer, they have taken up the shape
of a sphere. No other solid shape has all its parts as close together, on
the average, as a sphere does, which is why the Earth, a rac ng
everything to its center, is a sphere.
4. DOES THE EARTH MOVE?

To most people in ancient mes, this must have seemed the silliest
ques on imaginable; how could there be any doubt over the ma er?
We can see that the Earth simply does not move. To ask the ques on
at all must have seemed a sign of insanity.

Then why did people ask it?
One reason is because everything in the sky moves. The sun rises in
the east, moves across the sky, and sets in the west. So does the
moon. The stars seem to turn in vast circles about the North Star at
the center. Stars that are not close to the North Star make circles large
enough to intersect the horizon, so they, too, rise in the east and set in
the west.
This mo on in the sky didn't surprise most people. To them it
seemed natural that the Earth remained absolutely s ll and
mo onless and the objects in the sky turned about it, making one turn
every day. That was the way it looked, and why should anyone doubt
the evidence of the senses? But some people were bound to wonder
whether it wasn't possible that the sky was standing s ll and the Earth
was turning under it. To most, that didn't seem a reasonable
alterna ve. It was simply too obvious that the huge Earth was not
moving.
But have you ever been on a train, with another train just next to
yours, when suddenly the train next to yours started moving slowly
backward? You may have been astonished. Why should it be moving
backward? You con nue watching and, finally, it moves so far
backward that the front of that train moves behind your window and
you are looking out at the scenery and, behold, the scenery is moving
backward, too! You understand at once that it was your train that was
moving forward and the other one that was standing s ll. As long as
your train's mo on was very smooth, you couldn't tell the difference;
you couldn't tell which train was moving and which was standing s ll.

The ancients didn't have our advantage, however; they weren't
accustomed to travel being so smooth that they couldn't tell if they
were moving. Walking, running, being pulled in a springless wagon
over ru ed roads, or riding on a tro ng or galloping horse, all produce
such irregulari es of mo on that there could never be any ques on as
to whether you were moving or not. Therefore since the Earth didn't
feel as though it were moving, the conclusion was that it was simply
not moving.

Now suppose we imagine ourselves back on our train, watching the
train next to us moving slowly backward. To check whether it was
moving or you were, all you would have to do would be to look in the
other direc on. Out the window on the other side of the train you
would see the sta on or a town street. If it was moving backward, too,
then you would know that it was you that were moving, not the other
train. In the case of the Earth and the sky, however, there is nothing
neutral to look at.
The first person we know of who suggested that it might be the
Earth that was turning and not the sky was the Greek philosopher
Heracleides (c. 390?c. 322 B.C.) in about 350 B.C. He wasn't taken
seriously. In 1609, however, an Italian scien st, Galileo Galilei (1564?
1642), turned a very primi ve telescope on the sky. Among his
discoveries was the fact that there were dark spots on the sun. As he
watched them from day to day, he no ced that the spots slowly
moved about the sun and concluded that the sun was slowly turning,
or rota ng, about an imaginary line called its axis, making one full turn
in nearly twenty-seven days.

If the sun rotated, he thought, why shouldn't the Earth rotate, too,
once every twenty-four hours? There was s ll strong opposi on to the
no on, and in 1633, the Catholic Church forced Galileo to renounce his
views publicly and say that the Earth was mo onless.

But that didn't help the conserva ves, and in 1665, the Italian-
French astronomer Gian Domenico Cassini (1625?1712) was able to
show that the planet Mars was rota ng every 24? hours. In 1668, he
showed that the planet Jupiter was rota ng every 10 hours. A er that,
scien sts began to suspect that the Earth was rota ng, too; it just did
so in such a regular and smooth fashion that no one could feel it.
What's more, the rota on of the Earth did not depend just on the fact
that other worlds rotated; there was addi onal evidence. As
astronomers came to realize how large the universe really is (and we'll
get to that ourselves later), it became more and more nonsensical to
suppose that the Earth was mo onless and that the en re vast
universe turned around it.

It wasn't un l 1851, though, that someone actually demonstrated
the rota on so people could actually see that it happened. A French
physicist, Jean B. L. Foucault (1819?1868), let a long, heavy pendulum
swing from the ceiling of a church. It had a spike at the bo om, one
that just made a furrow in the sand on the floor of the church. The
pendulum kept swinging in the same plane for hour a er hour, but the
mark in the sand on the floor kept slowly changing direc on as the
Earth turned under the pendulum. For the first me, crowds watching
the pendulum could actually see the Earth turning. Nowadays, of
course, we have placed people on the moon, and from there we can
actually see the Earth turn.
5. WHEN YOU JUMP UP, WHY DON'T YOU COME
DOWN IN A DIFFERENT PLACE?

When astronomers grew insistent that the Earth turned, back in the
1600s, people who didn't believe that raised objec ons. If the Earth
turned, they said, then a person who jumped straight up in the air
would have the Earth turn under him and would come back to Earth a
small distance from where he started; if you threw a ball straight up
into the air, it would come back an even longer distance from where it
started; and if a bird flew away from its nest, it would never find its
way back. Since none of these things happened, people argued that
the Earth could not be moving.

These objec ons seemed to make sense, and if you had just learned
that the Earth was rota ng, you might be at a loss as to how to
counter them, and it would become necessary to think a bit.

Suppose you are seated in a train on a seat just next to the central
aisle, with a friend si ng right across the aisle from you. The train is
wai ng at the sta on and, having nothing be er to do, you toss a ball
across the aisle to your friend, who catches it and tosses it back--you
have no trouble doing this. Now suppose the train is not wai ng at the
sta on but is racing forward along the smooth, straight tracks at 96
kilometers (60 miles) per hour. You toss the ball to your friend--does
the mo on of the train affect the ball in the air, so that it doesn't reach
your friend but hits someone a couple of seats behind him? No,
indeed. The ball would go across the aisle just as if the train were
standing s ll. If you just think about it, your experience with the world
should be enough to tell you that what I have said about the ball is so,
without your having to try it yourself. (An exercise of this kind, one
that can be imagined without actually being performed, is called a
"thought experiment.")

Why is it as easy to throw the ball on a speeding train as on a
sta onary train? Because as the train races along the track, everything
inside it is moving at that speed, too--you, your friend, the air
between you, and the ball you throw across the aisle. If everything is
moving forward at the same rate of speed, then it doesn't ma er
whether that speed is 96 kilometers per hour or zero kilometers per
hour.

The Earth rotates at a speed of about 1,600 kilometers (1,000 miles)
an hour at the equator, but you and I and the air and any thrown ball
moves at the same speed, so you can play baseball anywhere on the
planet without worrying about the Earth's mo on.

The ancients, of course, didn't have anything like a train, so Galileo
used a different thought experiment. Imagine that you are on a sailing
ship, which is moving across the sea, scudding before the wind. You
climb to the top of the ship's main mast and drop a marlinespike or
some other tool that sailors use. The tool falls, but while it is falling,
the ship is moving forward so swi ly that by the me it reaches the
level of the ship's deck, the ship may have sailed ahead, leaving the
tool to drop into the ocean behind it.

There have, however, been thousands of sailing ships on which
sailors have accidentally dropped thousands of tools from the tops of
masts, and it is common knowledge that the tools never drop into the
ocean. They invariably drop to the bo om of the mast. While they are
falling, they move forward with the ship.

So this type of argument against the Earth's rota on doesn't work.
In fact, nobody has ever advanced a single successful argument against
the Earth's turning. It turns!
6. WHAT MAKES THE WIND BLOW?

If the air moves with the Earth as it turns, why is there wind? Wind,
a er all, is moving air, and perhaps it seems to move because, in
actual fact, it is standing s ll and the Earth is moving under it.

Unfortunately, that hypothesis is wrong.
The Earth is turning west to east, which is why all objects in the sky
seem to be moving east to west, just as the train next to us seems to
be moving backward when we are moving forward. What's more, it is
turning west to east at 1,600 kilometers (1,000 miles) per hour at the
equator. At distances north and south of the equator, the Earth moves
at lower speeds because points on the Earth's surface to the north and
south of the equator are traveling through smaller circles in the same
amount of me. (At the North and South poles, there is no movement
at all.)
If the air was standing s ll as the Earth turned, we would experience
a steady wind going from east to west at the equator at speeds of
about 1,600 kilometers (1,000 miles) per hour. Elsewhere, the air
speeds would be lower. None of this occurs, so wind can't be caused
primarily by Earth's rota on.
When Columbus sailed across the Atlan c in 1492, he found that
there were steady winds (now called the trade winds) blowing from
the east carrying him along. On his way back he sailed northward ll
he found steady winds blowing from the west (the westerlies) that
carried him home. This discovery was important, since un l then,
Western sailors had thought of winds as erra c forces whose
presence, absence, and direc on depended en rely on the will of
divine beings. A er Columbus's voyage it became clear that the winds
blow according to rule and that they could be exploited in carrying out
ocean trade (which is why they were called trade winds). What was
not known at the me was why the winds behaved in such an orderly
way.

The first hint of an answer came in 1686. The English scien st
Edmund Halley (1656?1742) pointed out that if all the Earth's
atmosphere were heated to the same temperature, the air would rest
more or less quietly on Earth's surface and there would be no winds to
speak of. However, the sun is ho est in the tropics, and the air there is
heated to a higher temperature than the air farther north and south.
Heated air expands, becomes lighter, and rises, while the cooler air
from the north and south moves in to replace it. It is this cooler air
coming in that forms the trade winds.

One would think that the cooler air would come in directly from the
north in the regions north of the equator and directly from the south
in the regions south of the equator, but that is not so. The trade winds
north of the equator come in from the northeast, while those south of
the equator come in from the southeast.

Halley couldn't explain this phenomenon, but in 1735, a Bri sh
lawyer, George Hadley (1685?1768), did. Cool air from the north is
moving at a slower speed than air at the equator, and as this cool air
comes south, it retains this slowness, while losing velocity to the faster
mo on of the Earth, which is moving from west to east. As a result,
the wind seems to blow from the northeast. The same effect of the
Earth's mo on and slower-moving winds to the south of the equator
makes the wind seem to blow from the southeast.

Conversely, when air from the equator is forced northward, it is
moving faster than the ground to the north and gains upon it, which
makes the wind seem to come from the west; this produces the
westerlies.

This system was worked out in mathema cal detail in 1835 by a
French physicist, Gaspard Gustave de Coriolis (1792?1843). Thus, this
change in the wind's direc on due to the varying rota on speeds of
different parts of the Earth is known as the Coriolis effect. This effect
can make the air whirl in circles with different degrees of force,
crea ng ordinary storms, hurricanes, and even tornadoes.

Winds are important. They air-condi on the Earth, distribu ng heat
so that hot areas are not as hot as they might be otherwise and cold
areas are not as cold. They carry off water vapor from the ocean as
they heat up and they release the water, as rain, when they cool
down, so that the con nents receive the fresh water that allows life on
land to exist.

If we completely understood the rules governing air movements
and wind, we could accurately predict the weather, including hot and
cold spells, rain, storms, and so on. The trouble is that the rules are so
complicated that, even to this day, weather predic on is imperfect.

In fact, we may never be able to predict the weather flawlessly,
because we can never measure the ini al condi ons precisely enough,
and even the niest change in these condi ons can result in enormous
differences in the final consequences. This situa on is known as chaos,
and it is becoming apparent that more and more natural phenomena
have chao c proper es that cannot be easily predicted, or even
predicted at all. This points up a deficiency in science and a limita on
of human knowledge, but if there are indeed limita ons to what we
can do, it is wise to at least know what the limita ons are.
7. WHY IS SUMMER WARMER THAN WINTER?

In the previous sec on I pointed out that temperatures are higher in
the tropics than elsewhere on Earth. That is because the sun shines
directly down on the tropics so that they receive solar heat in its most
concentrated form. Farther north and south, the sunlight comes in at a
slant and is spread over a larger area so that the heat is less
concentrated.

Nevertheless, people living in northern la tudes like, say, the United
States or Europe know that the weather gets ho er and colder
without anyone having to leave home. It is a good deal warmer in July
and August than in January and February. (In the Southern
Hemisphere, the situa on is reversed.) The easiest explana on would
be that the sun is closer to the Earth in summer and therefore burns
down on us more hotly--but that isn't true. The sun delivers about the
same heat all year long.

It is the posi on of the sun in the sky that counts. If the sun was
always shining down directly on the equator, then at noon at any spot
along the equator it would always be exactly overhead. Places north of
the equator would always see it in the southern sky at noon me,
while places south of the equator would always see it to the north.
The farther north you were on the Earth's face, the farther south the
noon me sun would be, and the farther south you were, the farther
north it would be.
The path taken by the sun, however, is at an angle to the equator.
The noonday sun shines directly on the equator on March 20th every
year, and on that date, night and day are each twelve hours long all
over the Earth. This day is known as the vernal equinox, since vernal
comes from the La n word for spring, and equinox from the La n
word for equal night.

From day to day, therea er, the noonday sun moves northward
un l, on June 21st, it is shining directly over the Tropic of Cancer,
which passes just north of Havana, Cuba, at which me it stops and
begins to dri southward again. June 21st is therefore called the
summer sols ce (from a La n word for "sun stands s ll").

The sun then begins to move southward ll it is directly over the
equator on September 23 (the autumnal equinox) and con nues in
this direc on un l it is shining directly over the Tropic of Capricorn,
which runs just south of Rio de Janeiro, Brazil, on December 21st. On
this day (the winter sols ce) the sun stops dri ing southward and
begins advancing north again, reaching the equator at March 20th and
repea ng the same cycle year a er year.

People in the Northern Hemisphere watch the noonday sun rise
higher and higher in the sky un l June 21st, then drop lower and lower

ll December 21st. The higher the sun, the longer the day me and the
shorter the nigh me. In New York City on June 21st, there are sixteen
hours of day me and eight hours of nigh me. The situa on is
reversed on December 21st, when you have sixteen hours of nigh me
and eight hours of day me. The disparity between night and day is
greater the farther north you go, and in the polar region there is a
period around the summer sols ce during which the sun does not set
for anywhere from one whole day to nearly six months, depending on
how close to the North Pole you are. Similarly, there is a period around
the winter sols ce when the sun does not rise at all for an extended
length of me.

In the Southern Hemisphere, everything proceeds in reverse. When
the noonday sun is rising in the Northern Hemisphere, it is sinking in
the Southern Hemisphere, and vice versa. The summer sols ce in the
north is the winter sols ce in the south, and so on.

Naturally, the higher the sun is in the sky and the longer it stays in
the sky, the more heat it delivers, so that the Northern Hemisphere
gains more heat by day than it loses by night during the summer
sols ce. The maximum heat comes in July and August--even though
by then, the sun is beginning to sink again--because the gain
con nues to be greater than the loss during those two months.
Similarly, more heat is lost at night than is gained by day at the me of
the winter sols ce, and the coldest mes come in January and
February. In the Southern Hemisphere, this is reversed, with July and
August being the cold months and January and February the hot ones.

In primi ve mes, the sinking of the sun was watched with
par cular alarm, for to those who did not understand the irrevocable
nature of the sun's rise and fall, there was always the fear that this

me the sun would con nue to sink forever and eventually disappear.
Therefore the coming of the winter sols ce, when the sun began to
move northward again, was a me of relief and hilarious celebra ons.
We s ll have a successor of that celebra on in our Christmas-New Year
season.
8. HOW DO WE MEASURE TIME?

If we consider the seasons of the year, as we just have, the ques on
naturally arises as to how we measure me.

There are various aspects of me that are psychological and
physiological. Thus, me seems to move more slowly when we are ill
than when we are well, when we are in pain than when we are not,
when we are sad or bored than when we are happy and busy, when
we do something we hate than when we do something we love. No
ma er how inconsistently me seems to rush or creep by, however, it
always moves forward. What's more, we all have the feeling that there
is an objec ve aspect to me, that no ma er how it seems to us,
regardless of our state of mind and body, me is really progressing at a
steady and unchanging rate. It is this physical me that we are
interested in measuring.

Imagine that you had no devices to tell me, but needed to keep
track of it. Surely, the logical way--indeed the only way--to measure
its passage would be to find some change that was regular and
repe ve and count the number of mes it occurred. One such
change that would have been no ced quite early on by primi ve
humanity would be the ceaseless alterna on of day and night. The
days can be easily counted and referred to, and no one has any trouble
understanding the meaning of expressions like "today," "tomorrow,"
"yesterday," "last night," "three days ago," and "five days from now."
Coun ng days becomes inconvenient, though, if you want to deal
with long periods of me. It is to easy to lose count. Another change,
not so obvious, but s ll well known in prehistoric mes, was the
changing shape of the moon (its phases from a Greek word meaning
appearance) from night to night. The moon changes from a thin
crescent to a full moon and back to a thin crescent, then does the
same thing again and again. (We will consider why this happens later
in the book.) A complete cycle of the moon takes 29? days, which is
known as the lunar month.

The succession of these months can be counted and used as a
calendar, the word calendar coming from a La n word for
proclama on, because the Roman priests would proclaim the night
that a new crescent appeared in the sky to mark the beginning of a
new month. This is a lunar calendar, the word lunar coming from the
La n word for moon.

The lunar month averaged 29? days, with alternate months of 29
and 30 days. Twelve of these months were almost long enough to
match the cycle of the seasons from spring through summer, autumn,
and winter to spring again.

The cycle of the seasons marks a complete year. The season cycle is
a li le fuzzier than that of the day-night cycle or the moonphase cycle,
but its length comes out, on the average, to 365? days.
As it happens, twelve lunar months do not exactly match the cycle
of the seasons; twelve cycles of the moon's phases are completed in
only 354 days--11 days short of the year. This meant that every once
in a while a thirteenth month had to be added to keep the cycle of the
months even with the cycle of the seasons. This was important,
because the calendar had to tell people when it was me to plant or
harvest, when to expect the rains or the dry season, and so on. The
Babylonians worked out a system for adding the addi onal month in
certain years of a nineteen-year cycle that would keep the lunar
calendar exactly equal with the seasons. That calendar is s ll used for
religious purposes in Judaism.

In ancient Egypt, a significant event was the flooding of the Nile
River, which spread new, fer le soil over the fields, something that
happened at intervals of just about 365 days. This was so important to
the Egyp ans that they made no a empt to keep to the changes of
the moon, and they made each month 30 days long. Then, a er twelve
months, they added five addi onal days and began all over. This was a
solar calendar, from the La n word for sun.

The Egyp an calendar was adopted by Rome in 44 B.C. The five extra
days were spread throughout the year, and every fourth year had 366
days to take into account the fact that the year was 365? days long.
With a few further minor changes, this calendar is the one we use
today.
9. HOW DO WE MEASURE TIME INTERVALS LESS
THAN A DAY LONG?

There are no regular and repe ve natural changes that force
themselves on the a en on of human beings that occur in less than a
day. Yet from ancient mes people have felt the need to refer to
frac ons of a day.

During the day me, this can be done by considering the sun's
posi on in the sky. We can speak of dawn, when the sun appears on
the eastern horizon; morning, when it is s ll rising in the sky;
noon me, when it is at its highest; a ernoon, when it is declining in
the sky; sunset, when it disappears at the western horizon; and
twilight, when dim light precedes actual night. At night, it's a li le
more difficult, but people who must work by night (notably people on
ships) can get a rough idea of the passage of me by the posi on of
the stars that wheel their way about the sky.

Surely, though, we would want to measure me even more finely, to
note the exact posi on of the sun in the sky. The trouble is that
observing the sun to determine its exact posi on will cause blindness.
So early man needed to work out a method for measuring the sun
without looking at it, and the solu on was simple. A er all, the sun
casts a shadow, and if you drive a s ck into the ground, its shadow will
be very long at dawn when the sun is at the eastern horizon and, of
course, it will point westward. As the sun crosses the sky, the shadow
will shorten and will be at minimum length and point due north (in the
Northern Hemisphere) at noon me. A er that it will begin to lengthen
and face in an eastward direc on.

By keeping an eye on the shadow, it is possible to follow the sun
me culously without endangering the eyes. Such sundials may have
first been used in Egypt about 3000 B.C. The s ck or gnomon (from a
Greek word meaning one that knows--the me, of course) was
slanted northward so that the end of the shadow would mark out a
semicircle, which could be divided into twelve equal stretches called
hours (from the Greek word for me of day). The ancient Sumerians
first used twelve as a common dividing number. The sundial worked
well in Egypt, where it is sunny almost all the me during the day and
where the length of the day does not vary very much in the course of
the year. Farther to the north, however, the varia on in the length of
the day is greater, and the days are o en cloudy, at which mes the
sundial doesn't work at all.

Of course, people could choose some other steady ac on that does
not depend on sunlight. For instance, they could keep me by burning
a candle made of a standard size and composed of some fixed
material. A certain length of the candle would burn in, say, one hour.
Or you could have dry sand dri from an upper chamber into a lower
one through a narrow opening, and know that when all the sand had
fallen it might mark, say, two hours. Such devices could work day and
night, cloudy or clear, and would be portable besides.
You could con nue keeping me by subs tu ng a new candle as the
old one burned out or by turning the sand clock over when all the
sand had dri ed out of the upper chamber. But, even so, these devices
had drawbacks. Different candles were bound to burn at different
rates, and even the same candle could burn more or less rapidly,
depending on such variables as air currents. As for sand glasses, the
sand dri ed through the opening faster when there was a greater
weight of sand above it than when there was less sand there, so it
could be used to measure accurately only the me of complete
emptying and nothing less.

Perhaps the best clock the ancients had (clock is from a French word
meaning bell, because the hours were announced by the ringing of
bells) was the clepsydra, or water clock, in which water dropped
through a narrow hole from a top chamber to a bo om one. The
earliest water clocks have been traced back to 1400 B.C., and by 100
B.C., they were made more efficient by having a continuous flow of
water running into the upper chamber, with an overflow. In this way,
the upper chamber always had the same head of water, and the rate
of drip did not change with me. Eventually, water clocks were fi ed
with li le floats that supported pointers that rose with the water level
in the lower chamber. The pointer thus automa cally indicated the
number of each hour as it passed.

Water clocks were messy, however, and every once in a while, water
was spilled and had to be mopped up. In the Middle Ages, therefore,
gravity was used. A heavy weight pulled down a cord that was wound
around a drive sha . The weight, as it was pulled down by gravity,
forced the drive sha to turn, and a pointer a ached to it marked off
the hours on a dial. The trick was to arrange the workings of this
instrument so that the pointer turned at a constant speed around the
dial once in twelve hours, or twice in one day. In about 1300,
something called an escapement was invented. This was a device with
teeth that engaged the turning drive sha and allowed it to move a
limited distance. Then it disengaged and another tooth caught it,
which helped the drive sha turn slowly enough and constantly
enough to measure a full day.

Even the best of these gravity clocks would tend to gain or lose at
least a quarter of an hour in the course of a day, so they had to be
checked against sundials periodically. That was good enough for most
purposes, but it was not good enough for scien fic experiments, which
might depend on the exact interval of me in which something
happened.

In 1581, Galileo (only seventeen at the me) was a ending services
at the Cathedral of Pisa and found himself watching a swinging
chandelier that was shi ing with air currents, now in a wide arc, now
in a small one. It seemed to Galileo that whatever the size of the arc,
the chandelier swung back and forth in equal mes. He tested this by
his pulse beat (which can't really be used as a reliable mer because
its speed varies with the state of one's mind and with physical ac vity).
Back home, he experimented by suspending weights from strings and
allowing them to swing in small arcs and large ones. In this way he
discovered the principle of the pendulum (from a La n word meaning
hanging or swinging).

The pendulum has a mo on that can, in principle, be used to make
the gears of a clock move with great regularity. Its two shortcomings
are that it must be kept swinging back and forth and that its beat is
not completely regular.
In 1656, a Dutch physicist, Chris aan Huygens (1629?1695), made a
pendulum swing between two curved guards that forced it to move in
a type of arc called a cycloid; its period was constant. He also worked
out methods for using weights to give the pendulum just enough of a
push to keep it swinging indefinitely.

Huygens's pendulum clock was the first mepiece accurate enough
for scien fic purposes. It could measure me to the six eth of an hour
--that is, to the minute--and for the first me, a mepiece could be
given two hands. The minute hand made a complete circuit every me
the hour hand advanced one hour. Since then, clocks have been
constructed that can measure me accurately to the nearest six eth
of a minute, or second, and a third hand, the second hand, was added.

Today even ny frac ons of a second can be measured accurately.
10. HOW OLD IS THE EARTH?

Now that we have dealt with the measurement of me, let's ask a
ques on about the Earth that involves me: How old is the Earth?

We know quite certainly that the Earth has been in existence at
least 5,000 years, for we have wri en records da ng back to 3000 B.C.,
when the Sumerians invented wri ng. We have ar facts, that is,
human-made objects such as po ery and statue es that date back
earlier s ll. Un l nearly 1800, almost everyone in our Western
tradi on supposed Earth to be about 6,000 years old. Those who
believed this did so en rely as a result of their interpreta on of the
words of the Bible, which they accepted as divine truth, but this was
faith and not scien fic evidence.

Naturally, there were some, a very few, who gathered evidence and
came to conclusions quite different from those the Bible offered. It
seemed to these thinkers that the forces of nature--rain, wind, the
ba ering of waves--were slowly changing the face of the Earth. They
thought that such forces could account for much of the present-day
appearance of the Earth, but only if they had had a chance to work for
a long me--much longer than 6,000 years. One man who thought
this, in about 1570, was a French scholar, Bernard Palissy (c. 1510?
1589).

Those who accepted a 6,000-year age for the Earth did not deny the
existence of change, but a ributed it all to the legend of Noah's Flood.
Palissy refused to believe that any such worldwide flood could possibly
have taken place and suggested that the Earth's appearance was due
to slow changes over long periods of me. He was burned at the stake
in 1589. It was a bad me for people who thought for themselves.

As late as 1681, an English clergyman, Thomas Burnet (c. 1635?
1715), wrote a book that supported the story of the Flood, but then, in
1692, he wrote another book in which he ques oned the story of
Adam and Eve. That ruined his career.

In 1749, a French naturalist, Georges Louis de Buffon (1707?1788),
began wri ng a long encyclopedia in which he tried to explain the
world in naturalis c terms. He es mated that in order for the Earth to
reach its present state, it had to be at least 75,000 years old. That got
him into trouble, and he was forced to recant, as Galileo had.

However, nothing, in the end, can stop people from thinking. The
turning point came in 1795, when a Sco sh geologist, James Hu on
(1726?1797), wrote a book called The Theory of the Earth in which he
carefully compiled all the evidence in favor of the no on of gradual
changes ac ng over a long me. Over the next half century or so,
scien sts came to accept Hu on's view--which was called
uniformitarianism--of slow, steady change. This theory does not rule
out occasional catastrophic incidents, such as gigan c volcanic
erup ons, however.

Scien sts then began to consider what changes were taking place in
the Earth at the present me and to work out how rapidly those
changes were happening. If one assumed that these changes had
always taken place at the same rate, one could es mate how long they
had been going on in order to leave the Earth in its present condi on.

The first man to a empt this was Edmund Halley, who had also
been the first to figure out what makes the winds blow. In 1715, he
considered the sal ness of the sea and reasoned that the salt had
been carried there by the rivers, which dissolved small quan es of
salt from the land they traversed. Moreover, he found that water can
be evaporated from the sea by the sun's heat, but not salt, so that all
rain that falls as a result is fresh water, which, as it feeds the rivers and
returns to the sea, brings s ll more salt to the ocean.

If you imagine the ocean to have been fresh water to begin with and
calculate how much salt the rivers bring into it each year, you can work
out how long the rivers must have been doing this to make the ocean
as salty as it is today. This reasoning sounds good, but it has its
uncertain es. First of all, perhaps the ocean didn't start as fresh water,
but contained some salt from the very beginning. Also, the total
amount of salt entering the ocean from the rivers each year was really
not known. In Halley's me, virtually nothing was known of rivers
outside of Europe. Then there was the possibility that the total
amount of salt being carried into the ocean today might be less, or
more, than the amount brought in ages past. Not to men on the fact
that there are some processes that remove salt from the ocean.
Ordinary evapora on doesn't do so, but some mes shallow arms of
the ocean are pinched off and dried up, leaving behind vast areas that
become salt mines.

Halley tried to allow for such irregulari es and finally decided that
for the oceans to be as salty as they are today, the Earth must be
about a billion years old. This was a figure that seemed so impossibly
large that no one could take it seriously at that me. It was over
13,000 mes as long as Buffon's es mate almost three quarters of a
century later, but condi ons were easier in Great Britain at the me,
and Halley did not get into trouble.
Another way of es ma ng the Earth's age depended upon
sedimenta on rates. The rivers, lakes, and oceans of the world laid
down mud and sludge, which se led to the ground and were called
sediment (from a La n word meaning se lement). As further sediment
was laid down, the weight of the upper layers compressed the lower
layers into sedimentary rock. People could es mate the rate at which
sedimenta on was taking place in the present, and if they assumed
that this process had been taking place at the same rate all along, they
could calculate how long it took to produce the thicknesses of
sedimentary rock that were found in the Earth. The results they
gathered made it seem as if the Earth had to be over half a billion
years old.

These es mates were of the roughest kind; they were sugges ve,
but they didn't carry convic on. What was needed was some kind of
change that was absolutely regular, that had been taking place on
Earth from the very beginning, and that could be easily measured. No
one could think what such a change could possibly be in the me of
Halley or Hu on, and when it finally showed up on the scene, a
century a er Hu on, it was discovered en rely by accident.
11. HOW WAS THE AGE OF THE EARTH FINALLY
DETERMINED?

In 1896, a French physicist, Antoine Henri Becquerel (1852?1908),
quite by accident (he was looking for something else) discovered that
a certain substance containing atoms of the metal uranium gave off
radia ons that were hitherto unknown. The Polish-French chemist
Marie Sklodowska Curie (1867?1934) studied the phenomenon
further and, in 1898, concluded that the new radia on was the result
of radioac vity. Uranium and another type of atom, thorium (which is
similar to uranium), were both radioac ve, and the Bri sh chemist
Frederick Soddy (1877?1956) was one of those who showed, in 1914,
that as a result of radioac vity, uranium and thorium atoms broke
down into somewhat simpler atoms, which in turn decayed into
others, un l finally, at the end of what is termed the radioac ve chain,
atoms of lead were produced. These lead atoms were not radioac ve,
so the process of decay finally came to an end.

Working with Soddy was the New Zealander Ernest Rutherford
(1871?1937), who showed that every radioac ve element had what
he called a half-life. In other words, a given quan ty of any radioac ve
element lost half of its atoms through breakdown in a certain
characteris c length of me, then half of what was le in an addi onal
increment of that interval, then half of what was le --and so on. This
meant that you could predict exactly how much of any quan ty of
uranium or thorium would be le a er a given number of years.

As it turned out, both uranium and thorium broke down very, very
slowly. The half-life of uranium was 4.5 billion years and that of
thorium was 14 billion years. Those long half-lives showed why
uranium and thorium were s ll present in the Earth's crust even if
Halley and others were right and the Earth was a billion years old. It
also set an upper limit to Earth's age: If the Earth was a trillion years
old (a thousand billion), most of its uranium and thorium would have
already decayed.

In 1907, even before the ma er of radioac ve breakdown had been
completely worked out, an American physicist, Bertram Borden
Boltwood (1870?1927), suggested that if a rock contained uranium,
that uranium would slowly break down and produce lead at a fixed
rate. From the amount of lead that had been produced, one could
calculate how long the rock had lain there, solid and undisturbed.

It wasn't quite that simple because the rock might have had some
lead in it to begin with. However, lead comes in four different closely
related varie es, or isotopes, that naturally occur in certain fixed
propor ons. One of the isotopes is not produced by radioac ve
breakdown, and by measuring how much of that isotope is present in
the rock, you can calculate how much of all four isotopes were in the
rock to begin with. Thus, only the presence of lead in excess of that
quan ty counts in determining the age of the rocks.

It was not difficult to find rocks that were 1 billion years old, and
Halley's original es mate no longer sounded ridiculous. In fact, by
1931, rocks were discovered that were 2 billion years old, and the
oldest rocks that have been discovered, in western Greenland, are 3.8
billion years old.

But that just tells us how old the oldest rocks we can find on Earth
are. Earth itself might be older than that, but in the days before 3.8
billion years ago, volcanic ac on might have melted most rocks over
and over again, so that none have survived in undisturbed solid form
from that earlier period. Actually, scien sts have been able to solve
that problem in ways I will describe later in the book, and it is now
generally accepted that the Earth is 4.6 billion years old.
12. WHAT IS MASS?

In order to find out something more about the Earth, it is useful to
decide what we mean by the word mass, though before we can do
that, we must also ask what we mean by weight.

Weight is the result of the pull of the Earth on some par cular
object. Some objects are pulled with such force that it is hard to li
them against the power of gravity; such objects are heavy. Other
objects are pulled with less force, are easier to li , and are therefore
light. We measure the weight of any object in kilograms; 1 kilogram
equals 2.2 pounds in American measure.

According to Newton's law of gravita on, however, the pull of the
Earth varies with distance. Earth's pull acts as though it is concentrated
at its center, and we are on Earth's surface, 6,350 kilometers (about
3,950 miles) away. For the most part, we are not aware that the pull of
gravity changes. A er all, we always stay at just about the same
distance from the Earth's center. Even climbing to the top of the
highest mountain or descending to the depths of the deepest ocean
doesn't make much of a change, so we usually think of weight as a
force that doesn't change.

But if we were to move to a height of 6,350 kilometers above the
Earth's surface, we would be twice as far from the Earth's core as we
are now, and the pull of gravity would be reduced 2 X 2, or 4 mes. If
we could step on a scale at such a height, we would find that we
weighed only one quarter of what we weighed on the Earth's surface,
and if we went s ll higher we would weigh s ll less.

Isaac Newton, who worked out the laws of mo on in 1687, looked
for a measurement like weight that had nothing to do with the pull of
gravity and would therefore not vary with distance from the Earth. If
one object is heavier than another, it is because the first object is
pulled more strongly by the Earth; is there another way to judge this
difference?

Newton maintained that the speed of an object can be increased or
decreased or altered in direc on only by the applica on of a force, and
that a heavy object requires a greater force than a light one.

This is, actually, the common experience of human beings. Suppose
you have a basketball res ng on the ground, and you wish to set it into
mo on. That is not hard; a push with your finger will do the trick, and
once the ball is moving, another push will change its direc on or stop
it. Now suppose you have an iron cannonball, the same size as a
basketball, that is res ng on the ground. It is much heavier than the
basketball; if you try to set it in mo on, you will find it requires much
more effort, and once it is moving, you will find it takes much more
force to stop it or change its direc on of mo on.

The resistance of an object to changes in mo on is called its iner a,
and the quan ty of iner a that an object has is called its mass. Mass
does not change with the strength or weakness of a gravita onal field,
so scien sts prefer to talk of mass rather than weight. They say an
object is more massive or less massive, rather than heavier or lighter.

Mass, like weight, is measured in kilograms, or pounds (which is
actually a mistake, but scien sts are stuck with the habit). It can be
measured in one of two ways: by determining weight, provided you
take into account the strength of the gravita onal field you are
working with, or by determining iner a, in which case the gravita onal
field doesn't count. The two methods seem to have nothing to do with
each other, but they always give the same answer, so gravita onal
mass and iner al mass are the same, a fact that puzzles scien sts.
13. WHAT IS THE MASS OF THE EARTH?

The ques on of the Earth's mass poses a problem indeed. We can't
measure the iner al mass of the Earth because it is so massive that we
can't produce a force that will alter its mo on in any measurable way.
What's more, we can't measure the gravita onal mass of the Earth
because there is no way of weighing it. But you really don't have to
weigh the Earth. If you take an ordinary object and measure its
gravita onal pull at a certain distance, you can then compare it to the
gravita onal force produced by the Earth at the much greater distance
of its center to its surface. If you know the mass of the ordinary object,
you can then calculate the mass of the Earth.

The trouble is that gravita on is such an incredibly weak force that it
shows up in quan ty only with a huge object. We think of gravity as
strong, as something forceful enough to kill, but that is only because
we associate it with the tremendous Earth. An ordinary object like a
lump of iron produces so small a gravita onal pull that it can't be
measured. Or, at least, so it would seem.

A Bri sh scien st, Henry Cavendish (1731?1810), tackled the
problem in 1798. He suspended a light rod by a wire a ached to its
center, and at each end of the rod he a ached a small lead ball. The
rod could twist freely about the wire in such a way that a very small
force applied to the balls would cause the apparatus to move. Thus,
Cavendish was able to measure how much twis ng was caused by
various small forces.
He then brought two large metal balls near the two small balls, one
on either side. The force of gravity between the large balls and the
small balls twisted the wire slightly, and from the extent of the twist,
Cavendish calculated the gravita onal force between the two pairs of
balls. He knew the distance between them, center to center, and the
mass of each. He also knew the value of the much greater force
produced by the Earth on the same light balls at the distance between
the center of the Earth and its surface. From the difference in the
strength of the gravita onal pull he could calculate the mass of the
Earth.

He decided that the Earth's mass was equal to 6 X 1024 kilograms.
(That's 6 million billion billion kilograms, or 3.7 million billion billion
pounds.) We s ll believe that to be just about the correct figure, so
Cavendish did very well for a first a empt.
14. WHAT IS DENSITY?

It might seem to us that a large object would invariably be more
massive than a small one, but we know from experience that that is
not true. A large object made out of cork may be less massive (and
weigh less, for that ma er) than a smaller object made out of lead;
some materials simply seem to pack more mass into a given volume
than others. The amount of mass in a given volume represents its
density, so we can say that some objects are denser than others.

A cube of water that is 1 cen meter on each side (1 cubic
cen meter) weighs just 1 gram.*

(This is not a coincidence; the values of the two units were
deliberately chosen so that this would be the case.) Since 1 cubic inch
is equal to 16.4 cubic cen meters and an ounce is equal to 28.35
grams, the density of water is not only equal to 1 gram per cubic
cen meter but also to 0.58 ounces per cubic inch. The use of grams
per cubic cen meter is so much more convenient than ounces per
cubic inch that for density, at least, I will use only the metric system.

If we know the mass of any object in grams and also its volume in
cubic cen meters, we can divide the mass by the volume and get a
figure that will represent its density.

The ancient Greeks discovered how to determine the volume of a
sphere from its diameter, and since we know the diameter of the
Earth, we can calculate its volume. Once Cavendish had worked out
the mass of the Earth, he was able to work out its density by dividing
the mass by the volume. It turns out that the density of the Earth is,
on the average, 5.518 grams per cubic cen meter, and, therefore,
5.518 mes as dense as water.

*A cen meter is equal to 1/100,000 of a kilometer, and a gram is
equal to 1/1000 of a kilogram.
15. IS THE EARTH HOLLOW?

This ques on may startle some of you who have never for one
moment ever thought that the Earth might be hollow, but there are
many people who have believed it over the ages, and the idea has
inspired many stories and legends. A er all, there are caves in the
Earth, though they are only superficial. The deepest cave we know, in
the western Pyrenees, is only 1.17 kilometers (0.7 miles) deep, which
is quite shallow compared with the 6,350 kilometers distance to the
Earth's center. There are always people, however, who have the
fantasy of finding a cave that will lead them deep into the Earth's
hollow interior.

The no on that the Earth might be hollow dates back to primi ve
mes. In the Greek myths, giants who rebelled against Zeus were
chained underground, and it was their writhings that were believed to
cause earthquakes. The Greeks' Hades and the Jews' Sheol were both
supposedly located underground, and the existence of volcanoes
seemed to make it certain that the interior of the Earth was a place of
fire and brimstone, fit for tortures.
In the early days of science, some scien sts found themselves trying
to jus fy this religious no on of a hollow Earth. In 1665, the German
scholar Athanasius Kircher (c. 1601?1680) published the most highly
regarded geology book of its me, in which he described the Earth as
riddled with caverns and tunnels in which dragons lived. In the early
1800s, an American military man, John Cleve Symmes (1742?1814),
presented elaborate theories about the hollow Earth and maintained
that there were openings in the north polar regions where people
could find their way into the Earth's interior. This idea caught the
imagina on of people in the way that crackpot no ons o en do, even
today, and a er Symmes's me, there was a steady drizzle of science
fic on books about travels to the center of the Earth. In 1864, the
French writer Jules Verne (1828?1905) published the best of them, A
Journey to the Center of the Earth, in which he described underground
oceans, dinosaurs, and ape-men, and suggested that the opening to
the interior was in Iceland. Earlier, Edgar Allan Poe (1809?1849) had
wri en such a story, too, with an opening at the North Pole.

Of course, when the American explorer Robert Edwin Peary (1856?
1920) reached the North Pole in 1909, it was quite plain that there
were no openings into the interior in the far northern regions. But the
stories con nued nevertheless. The most popular of the hollow Earth
stories was a series wri en by Edgar Rice Burroughs (1875?1950)
about Pellucidar, the name he gave to the underground world. The
first of these was published in 1913.

And yet we've known since 1798 that the Earth is not hollow and
can't be hollow. As soon as Cavendish measured the mass of the Earth,
we knew that its density was 5? grams per cubic cen meter. (The
current figure is 5.518.) The density of the rocks in the Earth's crust is,
on the average, 2.8 grams per cubic cen meter. If the Earth were
hollow, and the hollow were, presumably, filled with air, then the
overall density would be less than 2.8 grams per cubic cen meter. The
fact that Earth's overall density is 5.518 grams per cubic cen meter
tells us that the interior must actually be considerably denser than the
crustal rocks. The Earth simply can't be hollow. There are many other
reasons why it can't be, but its density alone is evidence enough.
16. WHAT IS THE EARTH'S INTERIOR REALLY LIKE?

Since we know that the Earth's crust seems to have a density of 2.8
grams per cubic cen meter and that the density of the Earth as a
whole is just over 5.5 grams per cubic cen meter, we can see at once
that a por on of the Earth's interior density must be higher than 5.5 to
bring it up to that overall figure.

If we think about this fact a bit more, it might occur to us that this is
only to be expected. The size of an ordinary ball of ma er that we can
manipulate in the laboratory is so small that its gravita onal effects
are negligible. In the case of the Earth, however, there is an enormous
gravita onal pull tugging at its substance. If we suppose that the Earth
is rock all the way through, then the deeper layers of that rock must be
crushed under the weight of the outer layers. This weight acts to
compress the inner layers, to squeeze all its mass into a smaller
volume. The deeper layers would therefore have a naturally higher
density than the other layers, and that might solve the problem right
there.

But it doesn't. Human beings can put a certain amount of pressure
on rock and can calculate how much that pressure compresses it and
raises its density. It turns out that all the weight of all the outermost
layers of the Earth cannot squeeze the deeper layers into anything
close to the density required to achieve the 5.5 grams per cubic
cen meter average.
We can only conclude, then, that the Earth is not solid rock, and
that in the deep layers of the Earth, there must be other materials that
are much denser than rock. But what are these materials, and how can
we possibly find out anything about them? I have already said that the
deepest natural caves are of li le depth. The deepest oil well we have
drilled is 9.6 kilometers (6 miles) deep, and that is only about 1/67th of
the way to the Earth's center.

Are we completely unable, then, to learn anything about the Earth's
center? Actually, we are not. There are occasional earthquakes that
trouble the Earth's surface and produce powerful vibra ons that travel
through the Earth's interior in the form of different types of waves.
This wave mo on resembles the manner in which waves cross the
surface of a pond when it is disturbed or the way sound waves travel
through air. In fact, some of the earthquake waves, called primary
waves or P waves, have proper es of sound waves, whereas other
earthquake waves, the secondary waves, or S waves, have proper es
of water waves.

These waves travel through the Earth and can emerge at the surface
at a significant distance from the original disturbance. The first simple
instrument to study such waves, the seismograph, was invented in
1855 by the Italian physicist Luigi Palmieri (1807?1896). Seismographs
were rapidly improved in subsequent years, and in the 1890s, the
Bri sh engineer John Milne (1850?1913) set up a series of
seismographs in various parts of the world. There are now over five
hundred very elaborate seismographs distributed over the surface of
the planet.

From what the seismographs tell us about where and when
earthquake waves reappear, scien sts can plot the path they take
through the Earth's structure. If the proper es of the Earth's substance
were the same everywhere, those waves would travel at a fixed speed
in a straight line. However, since the Earth's density increases with
depth, par ally due to pressure and compression, the path taken by
the waves curves. From the nature of the curves, scien sts can tell
how much the Earth's density increases at various depths. At some
depths, the waves veer sharply in their paths, which signals a sudden
change in density due to a difference in chemical structure, rather
than the gradual change caused by compression.

The study of earthquake waves divides the Earth's structure into
three main divisions. The outermost layer is called the crust, which is
made up of the familiar rocks we know. About 32 kilometers (20 miles)
below the Earth's surface (on the average) there is a sharp change that
was first detected in 1909 by a Croa an geophysicist, Andrija
Mohorovici (1857?1936). It is called the Mohorovici discon nuity or,
more simply, the Moho discon nuity. Below the Earth's crust is the
mantle, which is also composed of rock. The mantle rock is denser
than the rocks of the crust, however, partly because it is compressed
and partly because it is a naturally denser material. But the mantle is
not dense enough to account for the high overall density of the Earth.

At a depth of 2,900 kilometers (1,800 miles) below the surface of
the Earth, the earthquake waves veer sharply again, a fact first
demonstrated in 1914 by the German geologist Beno Gutenberg
(1889?1960). This inner region of the Earth, the core, is, indeed, dense
enough to account for Earth's high overall density. Scien sts
determined the composi on of the core by no cing that P waves
travel through it, but S waves do not. The proper es of the S waves are
such that they cannot travel through the body of a liquid, while the P
waves can. From this we can deduce that much of the Earth's core is
liquid. (The Earth's crust, its mantle, and its liquid core bear very much
the same rela on to each other that the shell, the white, and the yolk
of an egg do--but this is merely an interes ng coincidence and
nothing more.)

It remains, though, to decide what the core consists of, for it must
be made of substances that are denser and have lower mel ng points
than rock. The most common candidates are the various metals, so we
suspect that Earth has a liquid metallic core--but which metal?

Actually, a likely answer had been given long before earthquake
data revealed the fine details of the Earth's internal structure.
Meteorites occasionally strike the Earth's surface (I'll have more to say
about them later in the book), and although most of them are rocky in
nature, some 10 percent are metallic. These are always iron, along
with the related metal nickel, in a 9 to 1 ra o.

The French geologist Gabriel August Daubr? (1814?1896) therefore
suggested, as early as 1886, that the Earth's core might be made up of
a nickel-iron mixture. This idea seemed reasonable, and most-
scien sts now assume that the Earth's core is indeed 90 percent iron
and 10 percent nickel, though there are currently arguments as to
whether oxygen or sulfur, or both, are also present in significant
quan es.
17. DO THE CONTINENTS MOVE?

Since we've men oned earthquakes, it is logical to consider what
might cause them, and in order to do so, we should first ask whether
the con nents move. Of course they move in the sense that they are
rota ng about the Earth's axis as part of the solid globe, but do they
each move rela ve to one another?

It might seem that the answer is an obvious no. How can the
con nents move? But even in ancient mes, there was a feeling that
con nents, or parts of them, did move, at least in the sense that they
would rise or fall. As long ago as 540 B.C., the Greek philosopher
Xenophanes (c. 560?c. 480 B.C.) pointed out that seashells were
some mes found embedded in the rocks of mountain heights and
that, therefore, there must have been a me when those mountain
heights were underwater. Mountains, he maintained, originally came
from material that was at sea level and was somehow thrust upward.
He was quite correct in this, but no one took him seriously at the me.

About 1889, an American geologist, Clarence Edward Du on (1841?
1912), revived the no on in a much more sophis cated form. He
maintained that the rocks making up the con nents were less dense
than those making up the sea bo om. The con nents, therefore,
floated considerably higher than the ocean bo oms, and they rose
