above the ocean surface. Mountainous regions rested on rock that was
even less dense, so that they rose above the general level of the land.
Du on called this phenomenon isostasy. But even if the con nents
and por ons of con nents did move higher and lower, there seemed
to be no indica on that they ever moved sideways.

Yet the map of the world is sugges ve. Once the American
con nents were discovered and their Atlan c shores were mapped, a
curious fact emerged, one which was first pointed out in 1620 by the
Bri sh philosopher Francis Bacon (1561?1626). If you look at the
eastern coast of South America, you will see that it fits remarkably well
into the western coast of Africa. It is impossible to look at the map and
not begin to wonder if Africa and South America were once a single
land mass that split in half, with the halves moving apart.

In 1912, a German geologist, Alfred Lothar Wegener (1880?1930),
took up the ma er in detail and pointed out that the two con nents
might have dri ed apart by floa ng through the heavier rocks
underlying the oceans. In fact, he theorized that all the con nents
were once a single landmass, which he called Pangaea (from Greek
words meaning all Earth), that broke up into the separate con nents.
This phenomenon he called con nental dri . In a way, he was right,
but the sugges on that the con nents floated across the ocean
bo oms simply wouldn't work. The underlying rock was far too s ff
for that, so the idea was dismissed as impossible even as late as 1960.

But a new idea arose. In the 1850s, there were a empts being
made to lay a cable across the bo om of the Atlan c Ocean, one that
would allow for telegraphic contact between North America and
Europe. An American oceanographer, Ma hew Fontaine Maury
(1806?1873), took soundings of the Atlan c Ocean to try to decide
what the best route might be for the cable. In the process, he
discovered, in 1854, that the depth of the Atlan c Ocean was less in
the middle than on either side. He believed that there was a plateau in
mid-ocean, which he named Telegraph Plateau.

It was very difficult to take soundings of the ocean floor. One had to
lower a weighted cable that would have to be many kilometers long,
tell when it hit bo om, pull it back up, and measure the length that
had been paid out. It was very tedious work, and uncertain, too, and
few spots could be measured properly in the course of a single voyage,
so that Maury's work was just a beginning.

In 1872, a Bri sh expedi on under Charles Wyville Thomson (1830?
1882) spent four years at sea, traveled some 125,000 kilometers
(78,000 miles), and made 372 deep-sea soundings with a 6.4-
kilometer cable. Nothing be er was done for another half a century,
but the expedi on s ll gave only the barest picture of the ocean floor.
But then, during World War I, the technique of echo sounding was
developed. It used ultrasonic sound waves, too high-pitched to be
heard by human ears, which penetrated to the ocean bo om and
were reflected back in a ma er of minutes. From the me lapse
between the emission of the waves and the recep on of their
reflec on, the depth of the ocean could be es mated. A German ship
first applied this technique to ocean explora on in 1922, and we came
to know the nature of the ocean bo om in detail.

The greatest explorer of the ocean bo om was an American
geologist, William Maurice Ewing (1906?1974), who took innumerable
measurements and showed in the early 1950s that Telegraph Plateau
was not a plateau but a long, rugged mountain chain curving down the
middle of the Atlan c Ocean, with some of its highest peaks ju ng
above the ocean's surface to form islands. In 1956, Ewing showed that
this mountain range extended around Africa into the Indian Ocean and
around Antarc ca into the Pacific Ocean. It was a world-girdling
system, which became known as the Mid-Oceanic Ridge. In 1957,
Ewing showed that there was a deep fault, the Great Global Ri , that
ran down the center of the ridge and made it look as though the crust
of the Earth was broken into a number of ghtly fi ng plates. These
were called tectonic plates, from the Greek word for carpenter,
because they were fi ed together as snugly as if a carpenter had done
the job.

Another American geologist, Harry Hammond Hess (1906?1969),
thought about the tectonic plates and suggested in 1962 that material
from the deeper regions of the Earth welled up through the Great
Global Ri in the middle of the Atlan c Ocean and pushed the two
plates on either side apart. The plate bearing Africa was pushed
eastward, and the plate bearing South America was pushed westward,
while the ocean in between broadened, a process called sea-floor
spreading. This concept was rapidly accepted by other geologists.
South America and Africa had, indeed, originally been part of the
same landmass, as Wegener had suggested, but they had separated
not by floa ng and dri ing but by being pushed apart by force.
Wegener had come to the right conclusion, but the mechanism he
suspected of causing this phenomenon had been wrong. The new
mechanism was correct, however, and all of geology is now
interpreted on the basis of plate tectonics--the study of the slow
movement of the tectonic plates.
18. WHAT CAUSES EARTHQUAKES AND VOLCANOES?

Earthquakes and volcanoes have existed from the oldest mes. They
have always had the capacity for great destruc on and have
succeeded in frightening human beings dreadfully, for in a few minutes
they can kill many thousands of human beings. The most powerful
known volcanic erup on in historic mes was one that suddenly
destroyed the island of Thera in the Aegean Sea, north of the island of
Crete, in about 1500 B.C. The island had shown no signs of volcanic
poten al in the memory of the people living there, but deep inside the
Earth, enough pressure had finally built up to blow the top of the
volcano away. Not only was Thera destroyed, an event that probably
gave rise to the legend of Atlan s, but the island of Crete was so badly
damaged by rains of ash and dal waves that its flourishing civiliza on
did not survive much longer. In fact, the whole eastern Mediterranean
fell into chaos, and the Egyp an Empire went into permanent decline.

A volcano in Italy near the city of Naples had also been quiet for so
long that people had forgo en its danger. But then, in 79, it erupted
and buried the ci es of Pompeii and Herculaneum. The great Roman
writer Pliny (23?79) died because he approached too closely in his
a empt to observe and describe the erup on. It killed about 4,000
people, but Mt. Etna in Sicily, the tallest and most con nually ac ve of
the European volcanoes, killed up to 20,000 in an erup on in 1669.
Another huge erup on took place in Iceland in 1783. In 1815, the
Indonesian volcano Tambora blew up on Sumbawa Island, and in 1883,
another Indonesian volcano, Krakatoa, did the same. In all three cases,
there was heavy loss of life. In 1902, Mt. Pel?e, on the West Indian
island of Mar nique, erupted, and red-hot, poisonous gases rolled
down the side of the mountain into Mar nique's former capital, Saint
Pierre. In three minutes, all but one of the city's 38,000 people were
killed. (The excep on was a convicted murderer awai ng execu on in
an underground prison.)

Earthquakes can be even more deadly. On January 24, 1556, an
earthquake struck in the province of Shensi in China that is supposed
to have killed 800,000 people in just a few minutes. In 1703, an
earthquake killed 200,000 people in Tokyo, and in 1737, another killed
300,000 in Calcu a. The greatest European earthquake in modern

mes took place on November 1, 1755, when an earthquake (followed
by a dal wave and a fire) destroyed the city of Lisbon, Portugal, and
killed 60,000 people. There was also a terrible earthquake in 1812
along the Mississippi River, near the present-day town of New Madrid,
but so few people lived there at the me that it killed no one.

What causes these phenomena? We can dismiss early theories of
vengeful gods, fire spirits, and so on. Aristotle thought that air was
trapped in various places underground and that earthquakes were
caused by occasional discharges of the air that escaped. But as people
began to consider the ma er of volcanoes and earthquakes, they
no ced that the large majority of them took place in certain areas. Of
the five hundred ac ve volcanoes on Earth, nearly three hundred are
to be found in a big curve all around the borders of the Pacific Ocean,
and about eight more are along the chain of Indonesian islands, with a
somewhat lesser number along the line of the Mediterranean Sea.
Earthquakes happen most o en in these regions as well, which
suggested that earthquakes and volcanoes were somehow connected
and that the same causes might be responsible for both.

The earthquake at Lisbon prompted a wave of scien fic research
into the problem and led to the se ng up of seismographs here and
there, as I men oned earlier. Then, in 1906, an earthquake destroyed
the city of San Francisco, and an American geologist, Harry Fielding
Reid (1859?1944), who had come to inves gate the site, no ced that
the ground near the city had slipped. One side of what looked like a
crack in the ground had moved forward compared with the other side.
Most people assumed that the crack had been made by the
earthquake, but Reid had a different idea: it seemed to him that the
crack, or fault, might have been there all the me. (We now call it the
San Andreas Fault.) With me, pressure built up to make the two sides
of the fault move against each other. Ordinarily the two sides were
held in place by fric on, but as the pressure built up, one side
advanced, rubbing against the other in a series of jerks that caused
vibra ons strong enough to raze a city and snuff out thousands of
lives.

Although Reid was on the right track, the cause of earthquakes
didn't become en rely clear un l the tectonic plates were discovered.
It was then understood that, as a result of forces deep within the
Earth, the plates were always moving very slowly, but at the
boundaries between plates the forces some mes induced a sidewise
mo on such as Reid had no ced in connec on with the San Francisco
earthquake. The San Andreas Fault is, a er all, a sec on of the
boundary between the plate underlying North America and the plate
underlying the Pacific Ocean.
Furthermore, at the fault lines around the world there are weak
points where hot rock from below can ooze upward and produce
volcanic erup ons, and when two plates meet head-on, the edges
some mes crumple, producing mountain ranges. The Himalayan
Mountains, the largest upthrusted region on Earth right now, were
produced when the plate bearing India plowed slowly into the plate
bearing the rest of Asia. Some mes one plate slides under another,
sucking the sea bo om downward and producing the ocean deeps,
which in some places can be as deep as eleven kilometers (about
seven miles).

It had proven impossible to completely understand the various
phenomena involving the Earth's crust un l plate tectonics was
discovered. It made the seemingly impossible simple--which is always
the sign of a good theory.
19. WHAT IS HEAT?

It would now seem reasonable to ask what these forces are, deep in
the Earth, that power earthquakes and volcanoes. Before we can ask
that, however, we must ask: What is heat?

We all experience heat and take it for granted. It reaches us
primarily from the sun, which is why we can feel heat in the sunlight
that we don't feel in the shade. To a lesser degree, we can experience
heat emerging from fire, electric light bulbs, radiators, or ke les of hot
water, and even if we don't know what it is, we know what it does: It
flows from one body to another. If we are cold and stand before the
fire, heat passes from the fire to us. If we stand there too long,
however, we may absorb too much heat and begin to feel ourselves
growing uncomfortable, so that we have to move away. If we place a
ke le of cold water over a gas fire, heat from the flame flows into the
ke le, and the cold water becomes so hot that it eventually boils.

You can mul ply these examples so that it makes sense to assume
that heat is a kind of subtle fluid that flows from one object to another
in much the same way that water flows. A par cular substance, under
given condi ons, contains only a limited amount of the heat fluid. And,
to be sure, if a ke le of hot water is allowed to stand on a cold surface,
it gradually loses its heat so that it eventually becomes completely
cold.

But then, in 1798, an American-Bri sh physicist, Benjamin
Thompson, Lord Rumford (1753?1814), was boring blocks of metal
into long, thick cylinders for the manufacture of cannons. He no ced
that the boring device heated the metal so much that it had to be
constantly cooled with water. The usual explana on was that as the
metal was shaved away by the boring device, the heat fluid was
released and poured out.

Rumford no ced, however, that heat con nued to be produced as
long as the boring con nued, and it showed no signs of being used up.
Enough heat poured out to boil large quan es of water, and if all that
heat were imagined as being poured back into the metal, the metal
would have become so hot it would have melted. In short, more heat
came out of the metal than it could have possibly contained while it
was in the solid form.

What's more, Rumford tried boring the metal with a device that was
so dull it did not succeed in shaving off the metal and therefore should
not have released any of the heat fluid. That was not so; actually, more
heat was produced with a dull boring device than with a sharp one.
From this crude experiment, he concluded that heat was not a fluid
but a form of mo on. He surmised that the mo on of the boring
device was somehow communicated to the metal, and that ny pieces
of the metal (so ny as to be invisible) picked up this mo on, which
was what he observed as heat.
As it happened, Rumford was on the right track. In 1803, the Bri sh
chemist John Dalton (1766?1844) advanced the view that all ma er
was composed of ny par cles, too small to be seen, called atoms.
Eventually, it was established that all ma er consisted of atoms,
usually united into groups called molecules. During the 1860s, the
Bri sh mathema cian James Clerk Maxwell (1831?1879) and the
Austrian physicist Ludwig Edward Boltzmann (1844?1906), working
independently, showed that heat could best be interpreted as the
random movements--whether moving through space or vibra ng or
spinning--of atoms and molecules. This explana on is the kine c
theory of heat (from a Greek word meaning movement).
20. WHAT IS TEMPERATURE?

Some things are ho er than others. A par cular ke le of water may be
only lukewarm, while another might be near boiling, and we can tell
the difference easily if we touch each one. In fact, we don't even have
to touch them; if we place our hand a couple of cen meters from each
ke le, we can quickly tell which one is nearly boiling.

Why are some things ho er than others? Is it that a hot thing
contains more heat than a cold thing? That idea appeared to make so
much sense that it seemed as if no one could argue against it.

Yet in 1760, a Bri sh chemist, Joseph Black (1728?1799), was able
to show that the mere quan ty of heat was not all there was to
"hotness," or to temperature, as it is properly called. Suppose you take
a piece of iron and a piece of lead, both weighing the same and both
equally hot. You place each into a separate container of cold water. In
each case, the metal loses heat, which flows into the water, so that the
metal cools down and the water warms up ll, in each case, the
transfer of heat is complete. You would expect that the water would
be warmed equally in both cases, but that is not so. The water in
which the hot iron is immersed is dis nctly warmer than the water
with the hot lead in it. Both metals were equally hot, and therefore
had an equal temperature, but the iron contained more heat.

Again, if you put a lump of hot iron into a mixture of ice and water,
the hot iron would melt some of the ice as it cooled down, but the
mixture of ice and water (more water and less ice now) would have
the same temperature as it did before. It is thus possible to heat a
substance without raising its temperature. Instead, the heat produces
other consequences, such as breaking apart the molecules of solid ice
and changing them to the more loosely held molecules of liquid water.

To understand this idea, we can compare flowing heat to flowing
water. Even though heat is not really a fluid like water, it does have
some similar characteris cs, and the behavior of water can help us
understand the behavior of heat.

Quan ty of heat can be compared to quan ty of water, while
temperature can be compared to water pressure. Thus, if water is
poured into a cylinder of a certain height, it exerts a certain pressure
on the bo om of the cylinder. A given amount of water poured into a
wide cylinder will not rise as high as the same amount of water poured
into a narrow cylinder. Water in a thin cylinder may therefore exert
more pressure on the bo om than water in a thick cylinder, even
though the total amount of water in the wide cylinder may be the
same as in the narrow one. In the same way, it takes more heat to
raise the temperature of iron to a certain temperature than it would to
raise the same quan ty of lead to that temperature. The iron is like the
wider cylinder; it has a greater heat capacity.

Heat does not necessarily flow from an object with a great deal of
heat to material with a small quan ty of heat, but flows instead from
ma er at high temperatures to ma er at lower temperatures,
regardless of the total quan ty of heat in each. Likewise, if you have a
cylinder of water with a stopper at the bo om and place it partway in
a bathtub of water so that the level of water in the cylinder is higher
by a considerable amount than the water level in the tub, the water
pressure in the cylinder is higher than the water pressure in the tub,
even though there may be much more water in the tub than in the
cylinder. Now, if you pull the stopper out of the bo om of the cylinder,
the pressure evens out. Water pours from the cylinder into the tub--
from high pressure to low pressure. It does not flow from the greater
water supply to the lesser, from the bathtub into the cylinder.

In the same way, a few drops of hot water are at a higher
temperature than a bathtub full of lukewarm water, although the total
heat in the lukewarm water is far greater than the total heat in the
small quan ty of hot water. Nevertheless, if you drop a squirt of the
hot water into the tub, heat flows from those few drops of hot water
into the lukewarm water of the tub, and not vice versa. Thus, because
temperature dictates the flow of heat, scien sts are more interested in
temperature than in mere heat quan ty.
21. HOW DO WE MEASURE TEMPERATURE?

We can easily tell when one substance is ho er than another simply
by touching and comparing them, but our sense of touch is not
accurate enough to gauge exactly how much ho er it is. In fact, there
is a familiar experiment that goes like this: Dip one hand in water that
is quite hot and another in water that is quite cold and let them
remain there for a while. Then dip both hands in water that is
lukewarm. The lukewarm water will feel cold to the warm hand and
warm to the cold hand.

In short, you are no be er off judging temperature by feel than
judging length by eye. You would want a meters ck for measuring
lengths and a similar tool for measuring temperature. You would want
some phenomenon that changed regularly with rises and falls in
temperature and then marks these changes off into convenient units.
Galileo was the first to try to devise such a tool. In 1603, he inverted a
glass tube of heated air into a bowl of water. As the air cooled, it
contracted and drew water up into the tube. When the room was
warmer, the air in the tube expanded and the water level went down;
when the room was colder, the air in the tube contracted and the
water level went up. By measuring the water level, one could judge
the temperature of the room.

Galileo's device was the first crude thermometer (from the Greek
words meaning to measure heat) and also the first scien fic
instrument to be made of glass. It was not a very good thermometer,
however, for it was open to the air, which meant that the water level in
the tube was also affected by air pressure, and that confused the
results. In 1654, the grand duke of Tuscany, Ferdinand II (1610?1670),
invented a thermometer that was unaffected by air pressure. A liquid
was sealed into a sizable bulb from which a thin tube extended,
neither of which contained any air. Liquids also expand as the
temperature goes up and contract as the temperature goes down.
They don't expand and contract as much as air does, but even a slight
expansion or contrac on can cause a sizable change in the liquid's
level in a thin tube.

The first liquids used for the purpose were either water or alcohol,
but neither was sa sfactory. Water froze solid and would not serve for
temperatures on cold winter days; alcohol boiled too easily and would
not do for measuring the temperature of hot water. In about 1695, the
French physicist Guillaume Amontons (1663?1705) suggested the use
of mercury, which was an ideal liquid for the purpose. It remained
liquid over a far greater range than either water or alcohol and
seemed to expand and contract with smooth regularity as the
temperature changed.

A German-Dutch physicist, Gabriel Daniel Fahrenheit (1686?1736),
devised a thermometer, in 1714, in which a thread of mercury
expanded upward into a thin tube containing a vacuum from a
reservoir in a bulb filled with mercury. He made a mark on the
thermometer at the mercury level when it was put into mel ng ice
and another when it was put into boiling water. He divided the length
between into 180 equal steps that are now called Fahrenheit degrees.
The temperature of mel ng ice is 32?F, and that of boiling water is
212?F. There is some argument as to just why Fahrenheit chose those
numbers, but he did.

The Swedish astronomer Anders Celsius (1701?1744) devised what
is now called the Celsius scale in 1742. The freezing point of water is
set at 0?C, and the boiling point at 100?C. The Celsius scale is neater
than the Fahrenheit scale, and the whole world uses it now, except the
United States, which clings, perversely, to the older system. It is not
difficult to change from one scale to the other, however, and in this
book I will give temperatures in both scales.
22. WHAT IS ENERGY?

Heat is merely one form of what scien sts call energy, which is the
name given to any phenomenon that has the capacity to do work. The
very word energy is from Greek words meaning containing work. We
must be careful, though, for scien sts use the term work in a highly
specific way that does not correspond to the way it is used in ordinary
life. Work, to scien sts, is what we have when we exert force over a
distance against a resistance.

If we li an object with mass straight upward for a meter against the
resistance of the pull of gravity, we are doing work on the mass in the
scien fic sense. If we hold it mo onless at a height of a meter, we are
no longer doing work. We may think we are, for we grow red, but
that is only because our muscles are expending energy in keeping
tense; they are doing no work on the mass. If you were to put the
mass on a ledge that is a meter high, the ledge would hold the mass at
that height for an indefinite period without ge ng red, and it would
not be doing work, either. Nor do I do work in thinking up the
arrangement of words that go into the construc on of this book,
though it certainly res me a er a while.

Heat will do work in the scien fic sense. It will expand mercury, for
instance, li ing some of it against gravity. Your muscles will li a mass.
A magnet will li an iron nail. Electricity, light, sound, and chemicals
can be made to do work under the right circumstances. So can any
object that is already in mo on and that therefore possesses kine c
energy (Greek for energy of mo on), or an object that is posi oned at
a height and that has a capacity to fall and do work in that fashion, as
weights do in turning the handles of a clock as they fall. (An object at a
height is said to have poten al energy.)

Are all of these different kinds of energy independent, or do they
have some rela onship to one another? Electric current will produce a
magne c effect, and magne sm can create an electric current. An
electric current can produce sound in a bell, light and heat in an
incandescent bulb, and mo on in a motor. Light can produce
electricity, and sound can produce mo on. In fact, any form of energy
can be converted into any other form of energy; it is a single
phenomenon that can assume a variety of forms.

But when one form of energy is converted into another form, is any
of it lost in the process? Or is any of it lost when it remains in just one
form? The answer to these ques ons seemed in earlier mes to be a
resounding yes. The kind of energy that was the most familiar and the
most studied was the kine c energy of mo on. A massive cannonball
moving quickly enough to ba er down the walls of a castle is certainly
an expressive demonstra on of energy. But if the cannonball is
allowed to move across the ground, it does not roll on forever. It
gradually slows down and finally comes to a halt, and as the mo on
slows, the energy content of the cannonball decreases. What
happened to it? As nearly as any one could tell, the energy simply
disappeared.

It took me for scien sts to realize that whenever energy seemed to
disappear, it was converted to heat. The rolling cannonball's energy
was converted to heat as it rolled, but the heat was spread over such a
long strip of ground that it was unno ceable. If such heat is taken into
considera on, is there any loss of energy in the conversion of one form
of energy into another?

The first person to put this problem to a careful and persistent
series of tests was the Bri sh physicist James Presco Joule (1818?
1889). During the 1840s, he conducted innumerable tests in which he
converted one form of energy into another. He measured the original
energy content and the energy that was produced, including heat, and
concluded that no energy was either lost or gained in these processes.
He described his experiments and conclusions in 1847, but he was an
amateur scien st (he was a brewer by profession) and wasn't taken
sufficiently seriously.

That same year, however, a German physicist, Hermann L. F. von
Helmholtz (1821?1894), also announced this same conclusion. He was
a professor and his analysis of the theory was so carefully done that it
a racted a en on. Therefore he is usually considered to be the man
who first pronounced the law of conserva on of energy, which states
that energy can be neither created nor destroyed, though it can
change its form. Another way of pu ng it is that "the total energy
content of the universe is constant." Some people regard this concept
as the most fundamental of all the laws of nature.

Since studies of energy are usually reduced to the study of the flow
of heat, the science of the interchange of work and energy is called
thermodynamics (from Greek words meaning the movement of heat).
The law of conserva on of energy is some mes called the first law of
thermodynamics.

The importance of this law (and of all similar laws) is that it sets
limits on what is possible. No ma er what phenomenon is observed or
suggested, the ques on must be asked: "Where does the energy come
from and where does it go?" If this ques on cannot be answered, then
there is something wrong; either an assump on is unjus fied, or an
observa on is mistaken, or informa on is incomplete.

On the other hand, the law of conserva on of energy, and other
similar grand generaliza ons, cannot be proved. All we can say is that
scien sts have not yet been able to find any excep ons. Excep ons
may suddenly and unexpectedly arise that may force us to rethink this
law, to alter, broaden, or replace it. But a er a century and a half, the
law of conserva on of energy has held firm.

S ll, even the most steadfast laws of science are subject to revision.
As late as 1900, the status of nuclear energy was unappreciated, and
all considera ons of energy conserva on were incomplete without it.
Also, the no on that mass itself is a very concentrated form of energy
was not yet understood, and there, too, knowledge of energy
conserva on was incomplete. Physicists did not feel the loss because
nuclear energy and the energy of equivalence of mass happened to
play no significant role in scien fic inves ga ons of the 1800s. We
must therefore understand that even today there might be crucial
aspects of the universe we know nothing about, which, once learned,
will force us to recast our no ons. Nothing in science is ever beyond
improvement or modifica on, not even the law of conserva on of
energy, which is one of the traits that makes the game of science so
overwhelmingly interes ng.
23. IS IT POSSIBLE TO RUN OUT OF ENERGY?

The law of conserva on of energy makes it clear that energy cannot be
destroyed. That makes it sound as though we will always have energy
available to do all the work we wish. A er all, since using energy
doesn't destroy it but merely, at most, changes its form, we can (we
might suppose) use it in its new form, whatever it is, change its form
again, and so on indefinitely.

Unfortunately, it doesn't work that way. It is the experience of
scien sts that every me energy is used to perform work, only part of
the energy can be so used. The rest changes into heat. We can then
use the heat to perform work, but only when it is unevenly
distributed, when there is a region that is hot and another that is cold.
When this difference is used to do work, again only part of the energy
we are dealing with turns into work; the rest is lost as heat that is
more evenly distributed than before. Once a quan ty of heat is
completely evenly distributed, no further work can be obtained from
it. The result is that whenever we make use of energy to do work, we
end up with energy that is less usable for further work. Energy, as a
whole, cannot be destroyed, but the free energy--that por on of the
energy that can be made to do work--con nually decreases.

Another way of looking at it is to consider that all energy can do
work only when it is unevenly distributed, and not just heat alone.
Every me we do work, we distribute the energy a li le more evenly.
The measure of the even distribu on of energy (which can also be
defined as the disorder of a system--the more evenly distributed, the
more disorderly) is called entropy. The higher the entropy, the less
work we are going to get out of the energy. In this way, we might
visualize the universe as slowly, but inexorably, running down.

Everything we do raises the entropy of the universe. In fact,
everything that happens in the universe, even when human beings
have nothing to do with it, raises the entropy. This perpetual and
unavoidable increase in entropy is usually called the second law of
thermodynamics.

The first person to get a hint of the existence of this law was a
French physicist, Nicholas L. S. Carnot (1796?1832), who in 1824
published a small book on his studies of the ability of steam engines to
convert the uneven distribu on of heat into work. The ma er was
studied in detail, however, beginning in 1850, by the German physicist
Rudolf J. E. Clausius (1822?1888), who first envisioned the universe
running down.

But if free energy is always declining and entropy is always
increasing, how is it that, although the universe has certainly been in
existence for billions of years, it hasn't run down already? The answer
is that the supply of free energy with which the universe started was
so vast that even a er billions of years not much of a dent has been
made in it. The universe may be running down, but it will take many
more billions of years, and there is no need for immediate concern--
on that score, at least. In addi on, though we know much more today
about the ul mate end of the universe than Clausius did a century and
a half ago, we s ll don't know as much as we would like to. We are not
as certain now about the ul mate running down of the universe as
Clausius and his followers were then.
24. WHAT IS THE INTERNAL TEMPERATURE OF THE
EARTH?

Now it is possible to get back to the Earth and ask further ques ons,
such as what is the temperature of the Earth deep below its surface?

There has always been a tendency to think of the Earth as being hot
under the surface. A er all, there are hot springs here and there on
Earth, and there is the ferocious evidence of volcanic erup ons. It
might be volcanoes that gave early human beings the no on that the
interior of the Earth contains hell, a region of never-ending fire in
which the souls of people you don't like are tortured forever by a
vengeful and unforgiving deity.

There is no evidence for hell exis ng in the depths of the Earth, but
there is evidence that the Earth's center is a region of great and,
apparently, everlas ng heat. Once human beings began to dig deep
into the bowels of the Earth for such things as gold and diamonds, it
became quickly apparent that the further down people went, the
higher the temperature. In the deepest mines the temperature is all
but unbearable, even with air-condi oning.

Judging from the rate of rise of temperature with depth, it is
reasonable to suppose that the center of the Earth might be at a
temperature of 5,000?C. (9,000?F.)

But now that we know about the law of conserva on of energy, we
have the obliga on to ask: Where did the energy come from that
produced the heat? We will answer this ques on, but not ll later in
the book, when we consider how the Earth was formed.
25. WHY DOESN'T THE EARTH COOL OFF?

Even if we agree to leave for later the ques on of where the Earth's
internal heat came from, it is s ll possible to ask why the Earth s ll
possesses that heat. A er all, if it is 4.6 billion years old, why hasn't it
cooled off long ago?

By the laws of thermodynamics, heat should always flow from a
region of high temperature to a region of low temperature. It should
therefore flow from Earth's hot center to its cool surface and from
there out into space.

To be sure, Earth receives heat from the sun, which balances the
loss of heat from the Earth's surface, but even with the sun's heat
added, the temperature of the Earth's surface is, on the average,
about 14?C (57?F), and what is that compared with 5,000?C (9,000?F)?
Heat should s ll flow outward from the superhot center un l the
en re planet was at the same temperature as the surface. Although
the rocky surface of the Earth is a good heat insulator--that is, heat
flows through it only slowly, which greatly retards the process of
cooling the interior--it does not reduce the flow to nothing. It
certainly seems that in 4.6 billion years there has been plenty of me
for the Earth to cool off, and yet it remains a very hot body. Why?

It might be that the laws of thermodynamics are wrong, but
scien sts don't want to have to assume that except as a last resort.
First, they are bound to think that there might be a source of energy
available to the Earth that they haven't taken into account, and this
turned out to be true.

A er radioac vity was discovered, the French chemist Pierre Curie
(1859?1906), the husband of Madame Curie, realized that when
radioac ve atoms break down, they must release energy. In 1901, he
was the first to measure this energy and found, in essence, that when
an atom of radioac ve material breaks down, it gives off much more
energy than a molecule of gasoline does when it burns, or a molecule
of TNT when it explodes. Thus, a powerful new form of energy, nuclear
energy, whose existence scien sts had never suspected, was
discovered.

Radioac ve materials give off this energy so slowly that in the
ordinary course of events it is never no ced, but they con nue to do
this for incredible lengths of me. In the 4.6 billion years the Earth has
existed, only half its original uranium content and only one fi h its
original thorium content has broken down. In the process, the uranium
and thorium present in Earth's rocky layers have produced heat that
has been added to Earth's supply and kept it from cooling down. If
anything, the Earth has heated up a bit, an effect that will con nue for
billions of addi onal years, though it is very slowly diminishing.
26. DOES THE SKY TURN IN ONE PIECE?

It is me to turn to the rest of the universe, for that will help us ask a
few more ques ons about the Earth.

In ancient mes, the Earth was considered to be the en re universe
(in the natural sense, leaving out heaven, hell, and other supernatural
realms for which there is no scien fic evidence). All that existed aside
from the Earth was the sky--blue by day, when the sun shone in it,
and black by night, when the moon and numerous stars shone in it.
(The moon is some mes in the day me sky, too, and can be seen
dimly, despite the glare of the sun.)

The sky seemed to be and was therefore thought to be a solid vault
enclosing the Earth. To primi ves who thought the Earth was flat, it
was a fla ened semi-spherical lid that came down to the horizon
everywhere at the ends of the Earth. To those who thought the Earth
was a sphere, the sky was a larger sphere enclosing it, but was s ll
thought to be a thin, solid vault. The biblical term is firmament, and
the prefix firm- shows that it was thought to be solid; it is the
transla on of a Hebrew word that means a thin, metallic sheet.
If the sky were a solid substance, metallic or otherwise, it would
have to turn in one piece, so that everything on it would have to turn
in unison. But is that so?

These days, people don't look much at the sky because the large
ci es in which so many of us live are so garishly lit up at night that the
sights of the sky are largely drowned out. In ancient mes, however,
the world was truly dark at night, and when the night sky was clear,
par cularly when the moon was not in the sky, it was a glorious sight
of twinkling stars. Sailors watched the night sky so that they could
guide their ships by the stars; astrologers watched it because they
thought that certain details about the sky gave hints about the future
of na ons and individuals; and some people watched it simply for its
beauty. Those who watched the stars from places like Greece,
Babylonia, and Egypt no ced that all the stars wheeled in a circle
about the North Star. Stars that were close to the North Star made
small circles and remained in the sky all night every night. Stars that
were farther away dipped below the horizon as they circled but then
emerged again later. The important point was that they all seemed to
move together. They made up pa erns in which imagina ve people
could trace animals and other configura ons (the constella ons),
which remained u erly unchanged from night to night for as long as
people watched. It was as though the stars were shining li le spangles
that were pasted onto the solid sky so that they all turned together
and remained fixed in place as they did so.

For this reason they were referred to as the fixed stars. There are
about six thousand of them visible during the course of the night to
people with good vision. A few are quite bright, but most are rather
dim. But why were they called fixed stars? If all the objects in the sky
are fixed in place, why not take this condi on for granted and simply
call them stars? The trouble with that is that a few objects in the sky
are not fixed in place.

One of these is the moon, which is the most conspicuous object in
the night sky and must have been carefully studied even by prehistoric
people. The moon rises in the east and sets in the west, as the stars
do, but it lags behind them. It is impossible not to no ce that it
changes posi on in the sky rela ve to the stars, dri ing steadily from
west to east, making a complete west-to-east turn around the sky in
29? days.

The sun does the same thing, but more slowly. Of course, you can't
see the posi on of the sun among the stars when it is shining in the
sky, for then the sky is blue and no stars are visible. But once the sun
sets, the stars appear, and if you watched the situa on from night to
night, you would no ce that the constella ons all shi a li le bit
westward at each successive sunset. The easiest way to explain this
phenomenon is to assume that the sun, like the moon, is dri ing west
to east against the stars, making a complete circuit of the sky in 365?
days.

The sun and the moon are special bodies; they don't look like the
other objects in the sky, but are shining discs of light rather than
shining points. There are, however, five objects that do look like stars
(although unusually bright ones) that shi posi on among the
remaining stars. These five objects were first studied by the ancient
Sumerians about 3000 B.C., and they seemed so unusual that they
were given the names of gods. That habit persisted and was adopted
first by the Greeks and then the Romans. We s ll use the Roman god
names today, referring to these five starlike objects as Mercury, Venus,
Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn. These five objects, plus the sun and the
moon, are the wandering stars, and were called planets, from the
Greek word meaning to wander. (Nowadays, we no longer call the sun
and the moon planets, for reasons I will explain later.)

The seven planets have always been of absorbing interest to human
beings, because their wanderings are considered by the
unsophis cated to be a sort of code represen ng divine messages
about the future (a fact exploited by unscrupulous astrologers who
work out worthless messages for money). The seven-day week was
invented by the Babylonians to memorialize the seven planets, and to
this day, in many European languages the individual weekdays are
named for the individual planets. We have Sunday, Monday, and
Saturday (Saturn). The other four days are named for Norse gods. In
French, however, the other four days are mardi (Mars), mercredi
(Mercury), jeudi (Jupiter), and vendredi (Venus). The ancient Hebrews
adopted the Babylonian week and tried to put a religious cachet upon
it in the first two books of Genesis, but the names s ll show their
pagan origin.

Since the seven planets wander freely across the sky and can't be
fixed to the solid vault of the sky, the ancient Greeks reasoned that
each of the seven planets must be fixed to a sphere of its own that
turned between the sky (the outermost sphere of the stars) and the
Earth. Since those inner spheres weren't visible, they were assumed to
be perfectly transparent and were called crystalline spheres from a
Greek word meaning transparent. So the answer the ancients had to
the ques on of whether the sky turned in one piece was yes, but with
just a few excep ons, though as we shall see in due course, they were
quite wrong.
27. IS THE EARTH THE CENTER OF THE UNIVERSE?

This is another ques on that to some there seems no point in asking.
To the people of ancient and medieval mes, it seemed self-evident
that the Earth was the center of the universe. A er all, the en re
universe, to their way of thinking, was made up of the Earth and the
sky. It seemed as if the sky was always above us, at about the same
distance everywhere, curving with the Earth as the Earth's surface
curved. It enclosed the Earth, which was at the center, so where was
the difficulty?

The only uncertainty was the ma er of the planets. Exactly where
are they between the sky and the Earth? Since they travel at different
speeds, the Greeks assumed that the faster a planet seemed to move
against the background of the stars, the closer it must be to the Earth.
This is the result of common experience: When we watch horses
racing around a track, when they are at the far side of the track, they
appear to be moving rather slowly, but when they are on the near side
and gallop right past us, they seem to rush by like the wind. You can
observe the same effect by watching an auto race. Likewise, an
airplane traveling low in the sky appears to move along rapidly, even
though the same airplane, flying much higher at the same speed,
seems to pass by very slowly.
Judging by the speed of mo on, then, the Greeks decided that, of
the planets, the moon was closest to the Earth. Beyond it were
Mercury, Venus, the sun, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn in that order. Each
one had its own crystalline sphere, seven in all, and beyond them was
an eighth sphere, to which the fixed stars were a ached.

It was a very pre y picture, but it did not solve the problem of the
planets en rely. The ancients had to know the exact mo on of the
planets if they were to make astrology work. Astrologers (most of
whom in ancient mes were quite sincere in their beliefs) had to study
those mo ons very carefully and, in doing so, they gave rise to the real
science of the stars, which is called astronomy.

Even prehistoric people studied the sky closely. Thus, the stone
monument known as Stonehenge in southwestern England, which
came into being about 1500 B.C., might have been a device for working
out the future mo ons of the sun and the moon.

The stars move steadily and regularly, and if the planets did, too,
there would be no problem in working out their future posi ons (and
there would be no astrology, for the code represented by their
movements would be too simple to worry about). But the planets do
not move in a steady and regular fashion. The moon moves a bit more
slowly during one half of its course through the sky than during the
other half, and the same is true, though to a lesser extent, of the sun.

The other planets are even worse. In general, they dri west to east
(an effect known as direct mo on) against the background of the fixed
stars. Every once in a while, though, their mo on stops, and then for a
while they actually dri backward, east to west (which is an effect
known as retrograde mo on, from La n words meaning step
backward) before resuming direct mo on. Each of the planets has its
own pa ern of direct and retrograde mo on, and each one is brighter
at some mes than others.

These pa erns greatly complicated methods of calcula ng where
par cular planets would be at par cular mes in the future. A number
of Greek astronomers worked out ways of dealing with planetary
mo ons by supposing that different planets moved on small spheres,
the centers of which moved on larger spheres, some of which were
slightly off-center, and so on. It was all very complicated, indeed, and
the system was summarized in a book wri en in about 150 by a Greek
astronomer, Claudius Ptolemaeus (c. 100?c. 170), usually known as
Ptolemy. The mathema cal structure of the universe, with the Earth at
the center and various systems of spheres surrounding it, is called the
Ptolemaic universe in Ptolemy's honor, or the geocentric universe
(from Greek words meaning earth-centered). It was accepted by just
about everybody for 1,700 years, and hardly anyone ever dreamed of
ques oning it. But it was quite wrong, just the same.
28. ONCE AGAIN, THEN, IS THE EARTH THE CENTER
OF THE UNIVERSE?

There were, indeed, a few daring thinkers who ques oned the general
acceptance of the Earth as the center of the universe. The first person
we know of by name who supposed that the Earth was not at the
center of the universe, but was moving through space around some
other object, which was the center, was the Greek philosopher
Philolaus (480?? B.C.). About 450 B.C., he suggested that the Earth,
along with all the planets and the sun, moved about an invisible
central fire, which we couldn't see except for its reflec on in the sun.
This was a sugges on with no evidence or logical reasoning behind it,
and no one took it seriously.

A century later, about 350 B.C., a Greek astronomer, Heracleides
(388?315 B.C.), made a more sensible sugges on. He no ced that the
planets Mercury and Venus never moved very far from the sun, but
moved away some distance, then returned, moved away some
distance in the other direc on, then returned again, and repeated the
process over and over. He suggested, therefore, that what was
happening was that Mercury and Venus turned, or revolved, about the
sun, and that the sun, carrying Mercury and Venus with it, revolved
about the Earth. This made a lot of sense, but it was unacceptable to
most of the Greek astronomers, who were commi ed to the principle
that Earth was at the center of the universe and that everything
revolved about it, with no excep ons.

Then about 260 B.C., another Greek astronomer, Aristarchus (c. 310?
c. 230 B.C.), came up with an even more radical sugges on. This idea
arose out of his a empt to determine the distance of the sun. When
the moon is exactly at the half-moon stage, the moon, Earth, and sun
are at the extreme points of a right triangle (I'll have more to say
about this a bit later). This is exactly the kind of triangle that
trigonometry deals with; if you know the exact size of the angles of the
triangle, you can use trigonometry to determine how much farther
away the sun is than the moon. Unfortunately, Aristarchus had no
devices that would have made it possible for him to measure the
angles accurately, and his es mates were way off. Even so, he decided
that the sun must be twenty mes as far from the Earth as the moon
is, and since the sun seems to be the same size as the moon in the sky,
even though it is twenty mes farther away, it must also be twenty

mes wider.
From this informa on he es mated that the diameter of the sun
was seven mes that of the Earth, which was a serious underes mate
of the situa on, but it was enough to convince Aristarchus that it was
ridiculous to suppose that a huge sun would circle a small Earth. He
suggested, instead, that the Earth and all the other planets revolved
about the sun.
Aristarchus was the first person we know of who suggested that the
sun, and not the Earth, was the center of the universe (this idea is
known as a heliocentric universe from Greek words meaning sun-
centered), but it did him no good. Hardly any astronomers took this
no on seriously.
Nevertheless, as the centuries passed, astronomers grew a li le
frustrated with the complex mathema cs needed to understand the
geocentric universe. In 1252, King Alfonso?of Cas le supervised the
forma on of new planetary tables, which were called the Alfonsine
tables in his honor, and he said in exaspera on, "If the good Lord had
asked my advice at the me of the Crea on, I would have suggested a
simpler system of the universe." In the 1500s, it occurred to a Polish
astronomer, Nicolaus Copernicus (1473?1543), that there was a
simpler system of the universe--the heliocentric universe suggested
by Aristarchus.

Aristarchus had merely formulated the idea; he hadn't done
anything with it. Copernicus, on the other hand, followed it up, and
showed that a heliocentric system could account for the retrograde
mo ons of the planets without trouble, and could also explain why
they grew dimmer and brighter over me. More important s ll, the
heliocentric system made it considerably simpler to calculate planetary
tables.

Copernicus hesitated to publish his work because he knew he would
get in trouble with religious leaders who were commi ed to the
geocentric view, believing, as they did, that the Bible upheld it. His
handwri en manuscript circulated among astronomers, however, and
in 1543, the year of his death, it was published. (Even in a heliocentric
universe, the Earth is not en rely deposed as a center; the moon, at
least, circles the Earth.)

The first person to use the heliocentric universe to calculate
planetary tables was a German astronomer, Erasmus Reinhold (1511?
1553). These were published in 1551 under the sponsorship of Albert,
Duke of Prussia, and were therefore called the Tabulae Prutenicae
(Prussian tables). But though they were much be er than the
Alfonsine tables, now three centuries old, the old guard would not
give up. Most astronomers refused to abandon the geocentric
universe because they could not believe that the Earth was flying
through space. Some maintained that even if the heliocentric universe
produced be er planetary tables, it was merely a cute mathema cal
device, which didn't mean that the Earth was really moving about the
sun.

The dispute con nued for half a century un l Galileo and his
telescope se led it. In 1610, he looked at the planet Jupiter and found
that the telescope expanded it into a li le orb of light. That sight was
the first indica on that Jupiter might actually be a world. What's more,
it had four lesser worlds that clearly circled it, the way the moon
circles the Earth. Such subsidiary worlds were named satellites from a
La n term for the sycophan c followers of important people. The
moon is Earth's satellite, and Galileo had discovered four satellites of
Jupiter.

The importance of this discovery at the me was that it showed that
four objects at least were definitely not circling the Earth, but were
circling Jupiter, instead. This meant that Earth was certainly not center
of everything.

Of course, it might be argued that Jupiter circled the Earth, carrying
its four satellites with it, but then Galileo studied the planet Venus. By
the old geocentric theory, if Venus was a dark body that shone only by
reflected light, its posi on between the sun and the Earth was such
that it should always appear as a crescent. If the heliocentric theory
was correct, then Venus should show all the phases that our moon
does, from crescent to full, which, as Galileo found, is exactly what
Venus does.

This discovery just about established the heliocentric system. The
planets, including the Earth, circled the sun, and the term planets was
retained only for such bodies. In other words, the sun was not a
planet; it was the center. The moon was not a planet, for it circled the
Earth. The Earth, however, was a planet. Consequently, with the sun at
the center, there were now six known planets revolving around it in
the following order: Mercury, Venus, Earth (and the moon), Mars,
Jupiter (and four satellites), and Saturn. All these bodies taken
together came to be known as the solar system (from sol, the La n
word for sun.)

The adherents to the old system tried to argue that everything one
saw through the telescope was an op cal illusion, but that only
aroused laughter. In 1633, the Catholic Church resorted to force and
made Galileo say (under the threat of torture) that the Earth didn't
move, but that wasn't evidence. The picture of the solar system as
made up of planets (including the Earth) circling the sun has been
accepted by all educated people ever since Galileo's me.

Of course, the heliocentric system did raise some ques ons. The sun
seems to follow a path across the sky at an angle to Earth's equator,
thus producing the seasons. How does this work in a heliocentric
system? If Earth's axis was perpendicular to the plane in which it
circled the sun, the sun would seem to move across the sky right over
the equator. But the Earth's axis is lted at 23.5? to the perpendicular,
a slant that remains constant as the planet revolves around the sun.
This means that during one half of Earth's orbit, the northern end of
the axis leans toward the sun so that the noonday sun shines north of
the equator, and during the other half, the northern end leans away
from the sun so that the noonday sun shines south of the equator. This
view exactly explains the apparent rise and fall of the noonday sun and
the cycle of the seasons.

The major divisions of me measurement can now be seen in their
true astronomical form: the day is the period of the Earth's rota on on
its axis; the month is the period of the moon's revolu on about the
Earth; and the year is the period of the Earth's revolu on about the
sun.
29. CAN COPERNICUS'S VIEW BE IMPROVED?

Any scien fic view or theory can be improved; in science the search
for improvement is never-ending. Actually, Copernicus's view is not
very different from that of Ptolemy. It merely changes the center of
the universe from the Earth to the sun; around the sun are s ll the old
crystalline spheres. Instead of the Earth being surrounded by seven
planetary spheres with an eighth as the outermost sphere of the stars,
it is the sun that is surrounded by six crystalline spheres with a seventh
outermost one for the stars, while the moon has a special subsidiary
sphere of its own, enclosing the Earth. The calcula on of tables
remained quite complicated, and while the results were obtained with
greater ease and more correctness than before, many difficul es
remained.

The Danish astronomer Tycho Brahe (1546?1601) spent
considerable me studying the posi ons of the planets. He built the
first important astronomical observatory of modern mes and
designed instruments to determine planetary posi ons. He did not
have telescopes, however, which had not yet been invented.
Nevertheless, he pinpointed the posi ons of planets, especially Mars,
be er than anyone ever had before. He believed that his calcula ons
might make it possible to produce more accurate planetary tables, and
though he died before he could produce these tables, he le his
figures to his disciple, the German astronomer Johannes Kepler (1571?
1630).
Kepler spent years working on the figures and found no circles that
would fit the planets' posi ons properly. Then it struck him that if he
used an ellipse instead (a kind of elongated circle), it matched the
posi ons of Mars remarkably well. The ellipse, like the circle, has a
center, but it also has two spots, called foci, on either side of the
center along the longest diameter. These foci are located in such a way
that the sum of the two distances from any point on the ellipse to the
two foci represents the same length. The more elongated the ellipse,
the farther the two foci are from the center. Kepler was able to show,
in 1609, that each planet traveled about the sun in an ellipse, with the
sun at one of the foci of the ellipse. As for the moon, it traveled about
the Earth in an ellipse, with the Earth at one of the foci. This is Kepler's
first law, and it meant that at one end of the planet's path (or orbit,
from the La n word for circle, even though it wasn't a circle) the
planet was nearer the sun than when it was at the other end, and that
the moon was nearer the Earth at one end of its orbit than at the
other. Kepler's first law finally did away with the no on of crystalline
spheres that had been part of astronomy for two thousand years.

Kepler also worked out a way of calcula ng how the speed of travel
of a planet changed with its distance from the sun. The closer to the
sun, the faster the planet traveled, according to a specific
mathema cal rela onship (Kepler's second law). Then in 1619, Kepler
worked out a mathema cal rela onship that showed how long it
should take a planet to travel once around the sun if it was at a certain
distance from the sun (Kepler's third law). Kepler's laws of planetary
mo on made it possible to work up a model of the solar system,
showing exactly what kind of an ellipse each planet had and how their
distances from the sun were related to each other.
Of course, if the crystalline spheres did not exist, people were
bound to ask what held the planets in their orbits. Why didn't they
simply wander off into space? This dilemma was answered by the
English scien st Isaac Newton (1642?1727), who developed laws of
mo on and a theory of universal gravita on. He asserted that every
object in space a racts every other object according to a simple
mathema cal rela onship, a formula that elegantly supported Kepler's
laws and explained what held planets in their orbits. The picture of the
solar system as drawn by Kepler is s ll essen ally the one we use
today, and scien sts are quite sa sfied that no major changes will be
required in the future.
30. HOW WAS THE EARTH FORMED?

Now that we have a clear picture of what the solar system is like, we
can ask how the Earth was formed. We can't really consider how the
Earth was formed all by itself, for, as we shall see, it had to be formed
as part of a larger forma on, that of the solar system as a whole. So,
what happened 4.6 billion years ago that brought the Earth--and the
solar system in general--into existence?

One person who took up this ques on without referring to the old
biblical story (for which, of course, there is no scien fic evidence) was
the French naturalist Georges Louis de Buffon (1707?1788), who had
thought the Earth might be 75,000 years old. In 1749, Buffon reasoned
that the planets, including Earth, were related to the giant sun rather
like chicks to a mother hen. Perhaps, then, he suggested, the Earth
was born of the sun.

Buffon visualized the collision of the sun with some other large body
and imagined that the impact had somehow jarred a piece of the sun
loose, which cooled down and became the Earth. The sugges on was
interes ng, but it didn't account for the other planets, nor did it
account for the forma on of the sun; it assumed that the sun was
simply there.

A be er explana on was needed. A er Kepler had produced his
picture of the solar system, it was clear that it somehow represented a
unit. All the planets moved in almost the same plane (so that the
en re model of the solar system could be fi ed into a gigan c pizza
box), and they all revolved about the sun in the same direc on as did
the moon about the Earth and the satellites of Jupiter about Jupiter. In
addi on, they all rotated on their axes in the same direc on, as did the
sun. Astronomers assumed that the solar system wouldn't show these
similari es if it hadn't been formed all in one piece, so to speak.

The first sugges on that dealt with the forma on of the solar
system, rather than merely with the Earth, came as a result of the
realiza on that there was more to the starry sky than merely stars. In
1611, in the very early days of the telescope, the German astronomer
Simon Marius (1573?1624) detected a luminous hazy patch in the
constella on Andromeda. It was called the Andromeda nebula (nebula
is the La n word for cloud). Then, in 1694, Huygens (the inventor of
the pendulum clock) detected a luminous hazy patch in the
constella on Orion, the Orion nebula. Other nebulas were also
located. (The proper La n plural of nebula is nebulae, but in recent
years the English system of adding an s for a plural has been adopted.)

It seemed possible that such luminous clouds were vast
conglomera ons of dust and gas that had not yet condensed to stars,
and that all stars might have been nebulas to begin with. In 1755, the
German philosopher Immanuel Kant (1724?1804) published a book
that suggested just this. He thought that a nebula was slowly brought
together by its own force of gravita on, swirling as it did so. The
central por on became a star, and the outer por ons became the
planets. This proposal seemed to explain why all the planets should
move in the same plane and revolve and rotate in the same direc on.

In 1798, the French astronomer Pierre Simon de Laplace (1749?
1827), who, possibly, did not know of Kant's earlier work, described
the same idea in a book he wrote, but went into even greater detail.
He pictured the nebula as slowly contrac ng, and as it contracted, the
whirling of the nebula grew faster. This is not an idea that Laplace had
to invent; the contrac on is the result of gravita onal pull, which we
know works because it works within the solar system, and the
accelerated whirling of the nebula as it contracts follows the law of
conserva on of angular momentum, an effect every ice skater knows
who goes into a spin and then spins faster when he draws his arms
closer to his body.

As the nebula contracted and whirled faster and faster, its central
regions bellied outward and came loose. This process, too, does not
have to be made up; it is the result of the centrifugal effect, which is
well known from observa ons and experiments on Earth. Laplace
imagined that the por on that came loose contracted and formed a
planet. The central regions con nued to contract, and another planet
was formed, then another, and soon all were spinning in the same
direc on. Finally, what was le of the central region became the sun.
Because Kant and Laplace started with a contrac ng nebula, this
concep on of the forma on of the solar system is called the nebular
hypothesis. (A hypothesis is a sugges on that doesn't have the
strength of evidence behind it that a theory has.)

For a century, astronomers were more or less sa sfied with the
nebular hypothesis, but, unfortunately, they became steadily less
sa sfied. The trouble came from the ma er of angular momentum.
Angular momentum measures the quan ty of spin of an object, which
is par ally due to the object's rota on about its axis and par ally due
to its revolu on about another object. The planet Jupiter, rota ng
about its axis and revolving about the sun, has thirty mes the angular
momentum of the sun, which is a much larger body. All the planets
put together have nearly fi y mes the angular momentum of the
sun. If the solar system began as a single cloud with a given amount of
angular momentum, how did almost all that momentum come to be
concentrated in the li le bits of ma er that broke off to form the
planets? Astronomers couldn't reach an answer, and they began to
search for other explana ons.

In 1900, two American scien sts, Thomas Chrowder Chamberlin
(1843?1928) and Forest Ray Moulton (1872?1952), returned to a
version of Buffon's idea. They suggested that another star passed near
the sun long ago and that the gravita onal pull between them drew
ma er out of each. As the stars separated, the gravita onal pull on
this ma er caused a spinning mo on and set it whirling so that the
planets were given a great deal of angular momentum. A er the final
separa on, the ma er condensed into small, solid objects, or
planetesimals, and these further collided and combined into planets.
The two stars, having come together singly, each departed with a
family of planets. This explana on was called the planetesimal
hypothesis.

One difference between the two theories is par cularly important.
If the nebular hypothesis is correct, then every star might form with
planets. If the planetesimal hypothesis is correct, only stars that had
experienced a near collision would have planets, and stars are so far
apart and move so slowly compared with the distances between them
that near collisions must be very, very rare. The difference, then, is
between very, very many planetary systems for the nebular hypothesis
and very, very few for the planetesimal hypothesis.

As it happens, the planetesimal hypothesis didn't survive either. In
the 1920s, the Bri sh astronomer Arthur Stanley Eddington (1882?
1944) showed that the interior of the sun was much ho er than
anyone had expected. (There will be more to say about this later.)
Material pulled out of the sun (or out of the other star) would be so
hot that it could not condense into planets, but would spread out into
space. The American astronomer Lyman Spitzer, Jr. (b. 1914), showed
this convincingly in 1939.

In 1944, the German astronomer Carl Friedrich von Weizs?cker (b.
1912) returned to the nebular hypothesis, but improved it. He
pictured the whirling nebula as developing subwhirls that formed first
planetesimals and then planets. Astronomers could take
electromagne c effects in the nebula (something not known in
Laplace's me) into considera on to account for the way in which
angular momentum was transferred from the sun to the planets.

The forma on of planets from planetesimals, by the way, explains
the internal heat of the Earth. The planetesimals are moving quickly
and have enormous kine c energy, but as they smash together, they
come to a halt and the kine c energy turns to heat. By the me a
planet is formed, the heat produced by all the hal ng of mo on is
enormous, which is why the Earth's core might be as hot as 5,000?C
(9,000?F). Naturally, the larger a planet, the more kine c energy has
been turned into heat in the process of its forma on and the ho er it
must be at its core. The smaller a planet, the less kine c energy was
involved in its planetesimals to begin with and the cooler it must be at
the center. Thus the moon is undoubtedly much cooler than 5,000?C at
its center, since it is smaller than the Earth, and Jupiter, which is larger
than the Earth and, indeed, is the largest planet, must be considerably
ho er. Some es mates place the central temperature of Jupiter at
50,000?C (90,000?F). For now, then, the new version of the nebular
hypothesis seems to be quite sa sfactory.
31. IS THE EARTH A MAGNET?

Since we've men oned electromagne c phenomena in connec on
with the forma on of the solar system, it would seem that the bodies
of that system have magne c proper es. Could it be, then, that the
Earth is a magnet? Indeed, scholars have been puzzling over the
ma er for centuries.

The first person we know of to describe the property of some
substances to a ract iron was Thales (c. 636?c. 546) in about 550 B.C.
He described it in connec on with a piece of rock found near the city
of Magnesia in Asia Minor, from which the word magnet was derived.
Magnets remained merely a curiosity un l the Chinese discovered that
magne zed needles invariably oriented themselves, if given a chance,
in the north-south direc on. In 1180, the English scholar Alexander
Neckam (1157?1217) men oned such a magne c compass. It was
eventually used to guide ships over the ocean and led to the great
European Age of Explora on beginning about 1420.

In 1269, a French scholar, Petrus Peregrinus (c. 1240??), was the
first to study magnets systema cally. He found, among other things,
that every magnet has two poles with opposite magne c proper es--
usually referred to as the north magne c pole and the south magne c
pole. The north pole of one magnet a racts the south pole of another,
though two north poles or two south poles repel each other. But why
would a magnet's north pole point to the north? Was the Earth itself a
huge magnet? This possibility was inves gated by an English scien st,
William Gilbert (1544?1603), who shaped a lodestone, the magne c
substance Thales had first studied, into a sphere. In 1600, he published
a book in which he described the way a magne c compass acted in the
neighborhood of that magne zed sphere and showed that it acted just
as it does in connec on with the Earth. It then seemed that the Earth
must be a magnet.

But why? One possibility was that there might be a huge piece of
magne zed substance at the Earth's center, poin ng north and south,
and once people began to speculate that the Earth might have an iron
core, that seemed to be the answer. In 1895, however, Pierre Curie
discovered that iron lost its magne sm at temperatures over 760?C
(1,400?F), and since it is quite likely that Earth's core temperature is
well over that figure, it is obvious that the core is not a magnet in the
ordinary sense.

However, hot liquid iron will s ll conduct an electric current, and if
the iron swirls, the turning current would create a magne c field.
Earth could then be, not an ordinary magnet, but an electromagnet. In
1939, the German-American geophysicist Walter Maurice Els?sser (b.
1904) suggested that Earth's rota on could cause swirls in the core
that would produce a magne c field.

Although this is a widely accepted thought now, problems remain.
The Earth's north and south magne c poles are not located at the
geographic poles but about 1,600 kilometers (about 1,000 miles) away
in each case. If a line is drawn from the Earth's north magne c pole to
its south magne c pole through the body of the planet, it does not
pass through the center of the Earth. What's more, the magne c poles
dri slowly, and the magne c field grows more or less intense with

me. There are mes, in fact, when the field drops to zero, then
reverses and grows in intensity again. There is s ll much we don't
know about the detailed mechanics of Earth's magne c field.
32. IS THE EARTH A PERFECT SPHERE?

As we learn more and more about the Earth, it becomes possible to
ask more detailed ques ons. For instance, scien sts have known for
nearly 2,500 years that the Earth is a sphere, but is it a perfect sphere?

Why shouldn't it be? If the Earth is a sphere because the pull of
gravity draws its substance as closely as possible to the center, then it
should indeed be a perfect sphere. Besides, the sun makes a perfect
circle in the sky at all mes, and so does the moon, which means that
those bodies are perfect spheres.

The first evidence to upset that no on was the sight of Jupiter and
Saturn through a telescope in the early 1600s. Both seemed to be
ellip cal rather than circular in appearance, and they maintained that
shape as they turned. What's more, the longest diameter of the
planetary ellipse in those two cases seemed to be that of the equators
of those planets, which means they were both spheres that bulged at
the equator and were fla ened at the poles. Bodies of which this is
true are called oblate spheroids.

Why should Jupiter and Saturn be oblate spheroids?
The answer did not come un l Newton worked out his laws of
mo on and tackled the problem in 1687. Every par cle of a planet is
forced to turn as the planet rotates on its axis, although the natural
tendency is for an object, once it is moving, to con nue onward in a
straight line. There is a compromise, then; the planet turns, but the
surface bulges out slightly as it does so, as though it felt the tendency
not to turn but to move straight ahead. This is a centrifugal effect
(from La n words meaning to fly from the center), a phenomenon that
has been studied in detail here on Earth. The faster an object rotates,
the farther it bulges outward.

As a planet turns, the por ons of its surface near the pole make
very small circles in the course of the turn. Such points don't move fast
and therefore don't bulge out much. As one moves farther from the
pole, the surface makes a bigger circle, but must make the turn in the
same me. The points on the surface must therefore move more
quickly and bulge outward more, an effect which reaches its maximum
at the equator. Thus, there is a central bulge on a turning planet, one
that is deepest at the equator itself.

The size of the equatorial bulge of a world depends on how fast the
surface moves and how strongly the world's gravita onal pull resists
the forma on of a bulge. The moon, Venus, and Mercury all spin so
slowly that there is no equatorial bulge worth men oning in any of
these cases. On the other hand, the sun spins quite rapidly, and a
point on its equator travels at the speed of 13,600 kilometers (8,500
miles) per hour, but its gravita onal pull is so intense that there is no
equatorial bulge worth men oning, either.

Jupiter and Saturn are very large compared with the Earth, and yet
they rotate on their axes more rapidly. Jupiter rotates in a li le less
than ten hours, and Saturn, though somewhat smaller, in a li le over
ten hours. A point on Jupiter's equator travels 45,765 kilometers
(28,400 miles) per hour, while a point on Saturn's equator travels
36,850 kilometers (22,900 miles) per hour. These are faster mo ons
than those of points on the sun's equator, and both Jupiter and Saturn
have much smaller gravita onal pulls than the sun has, not enough to
resist the centrifugal effect. Therefore both planets have pronounced
equatorial bulges. Saturn's surface moves less rapidly than Jupiter's,
but Saturn also has a smaller gravita onal pull, and thus has a greater
equatorial bulge.

But if all this is true of Jupiter and Saturn, might it not also be true
of the Earth? The Earth spins on its axis more quickly than the moon,
Mercury, or Venus. A point on Earth's equator travels at a speed of
1,670 kilometers (1,040 miles) per hour, which is much less than the
equatorial speeds of the sun, Jupiter, and Saturn, but Earth's
gravita onal pull is much less, too. It seemed to Newton that the
Earth's equatorial bulge ought to be large enough to measure.

The way to test this theory would be to go to various parts of the
Earth and make careful measurements of distances and angles so that
we could tell how much the surface of the Earth curved. If the Earth
was a perfect sphere, it would curve equally everywhere; if it was an
oblate spheroid, it would curve more near the equator than near the
poles. In 1736, a French expedi on under Pierre Louis de Maupertuis
(1698?1759) went to Lapland, near the North Pole, to measure the
curvature there. At the same me, another French expedi on, under
Charles de La Condamine (1701?1774), went to Peru, near the
equator, also to measure the Earth's curvature.

Newton proved to be right. Earth had an equatorial bulge, though
not much of one. The Earth's equatorial diameter is 12,756 kilometers
(7,926 miles), while its polar diameter is 12,713 kilometers (7,900
miles). The difference is 43 kilometers (26 miles). In other words, the
Earth is almost a perfect sphere, but not quite.

In 1959, a satellite called Vanguard I was placed in orbit about the
Earth by the United States. From the precise manner in which it circled
the Earth, it could be calculated that the equatorial bulge was 7.6
meters (25 feet) higher south of the equator than north of the
equator. This was announced as proof that the Earth was pear-shaped;
that is, broader in the southern half than in the northern. It was an
unfortunate remark, for the difference in the bulge is measurable only
under the most delicate condi ons. In fact, the en re bulge is so small
as not to be visible at all to the unaided eye, and to anyone looking at
Earth from space, it would appear to be a perfect sphere. Calling the
Earth "pear-shaped" made some people think that as seen from space,
the Earth was shaped like a Bartle pear, which is an u erly false
picture of the situa on. Fortunately, the use of the expression quickly
died.
33. WHY DOES THE MOON CHANGE ITS SHAPE?

It is me, now, to turn our a en on to other parts of the universe. The
ancient Greeks felt, quite correctly, that the moon was, of all objects in
the sky, closest to Earth, so it seems fi ng to consider it next.

The moon is the only permanent object in the heavens that visibly
changes its shape from night to night. The sun is always a blindingly
bright circle of light. The other planets and stars are always points of
light. Some comets do have peculiar and changeable shapes, but they
are not permanently visible in the night sky. (I'll talk about them later
in the book.)

The moon, however, goes through a steady, progressive, and
repe ve series of changes. At some par cular night the moon
appears low in the western sky just a er sunset as a very thin
crescent. From night to night it dri s eastward and the crescent grows
thicker. A er about a week it is a semicircle of light and con nues to
get larger, un l a er another week it is a full circle of light. Then it
begins to shrink. It is back to a semicircle a week later (but it's the
other half of the circle that is now lit up) and, finally, a er s ll another
week, it is a very thin crescent in the eastern sky just before dawn.
A er that, it disappears for a couple of nights and then the whole
cycle starts over again.

The natural thought might be that the moon is like a living being. It
is born, grows, reaches maximum size, then fades and dies, going
