But that is risky reasoning. It would be much be er if one star, aside
from our own sun, were actually found to have a planetary system.
Unfortunately, even with our present-day instruments, we can't see
any planets circling other stars. Such a planet would be 4.4 light-years
away, even if it were circling the very nearest star, and it would be
shining only by the reflected light of that star, so that it would not
deliver enough light to be seen at that distance. Even if it did, the
much brighter light of the nearby star would drown it out. (The four
large satellites of Jupiter are bright enough to be seen by the unaided
eye, but the far greater light of nearby Jupiter drowns them out and
we can see them only by telescope.)

There is an answer, however. Sirius B was discovered by Bessel
because its gravita onal pull was forcing Sirius A to move in a wavy
line, not because it was seen through a telescope. Might a planet, or
group of planets, do the same for the stars they circle?

In theory, yes, though the effect would be extremely small. A er all,
Sirius B has the mass of the sun, whereas we would be fortunate if a
planet had a mass a thousandth of the sun. If there was more than
one planet, they would distribute themselves around the star and the
gravita onal effect would be partly canceled unless one planet was
more massive than all the rest put together (as is the case in our own
planetary system).

The best chance for detec ng a planet outside our solar system is to
choose a star that is very close to us so that we can measure any
devia on from its path most accurately. It should also be small, so that
a planet could affect its mo on sufficiently, and the planet itself would
have to be very large to produce a sizable effect.

The Dutch-American astronomer Peter Van de Kamp (b. 1901)
inves gated nearby small stars for just that purpose. He felt that he
had detected ny irregulari es in the mo on of nearby stars such as
61 Cygni, Lalande 21185, and, in par cular, Barnard's Star.

This last star was named for the astronomer Barnard, who, in 1916,
was the first to no ce that it had the fastest proper mo on of any
other known star--and it s ll holds the record today. It dri s a
distance equal to the diameter of the full moon in 180 years, which is
very fast for a star. Part of the reason for this is that it is the second
closest star to us, only 5.97 light-years away. In addi on to being very
near us, it is quite small, being a dim red dwarf, and Van de Kamp
thought that from its mo on he had detected a Jupiter-sized planet
circling it. He found similar large planets in connec on with the other
stars he studied. But his work was at the very edge of what his
instruments could detect, and later astronomers since have decided
that his results were not reliable.

On the other hand, in the last couple of years some bright stars have
been found to be surrounded by bands of dust. It is hard to avoid
thinking these might be asteroid belts, and where asteroids exist,
larger planets ought to exist, too. Nevertheless, we s ll have not
actually observed any planets circling other stars, and must be
sa sfied with reasoning they are very likely to exist just the same.

If, however, there are planets circling most stars, what does that tell
us about the possibility of life on those planets?

Life certainly can't exist on any world that is part of another
planetary system, just as it cannot exist on any world in our own
planetary system. The planet has to be suitable for life.

For one thing, a planet would have to have a reasonably stable
orbit. If it had an erra c orbit, there might be mes when its
temperature would rise above the boiling point of water or, at other

mes, drop below Antarc c temperatures, and there would not be
much chance of finding a flourishing abode of life as we know it.
What's more, a planet would have to be massive enough to hold on to
an atmosphere and an ocean, but not so massive that it collected
hydrogen and helium.

But even assuming that a planet is the right size and has the proper
chemical composi on and a stable orbit neither too far from its star
nor too close, so that its temperature is at all mes in the range of
liquid water (as is true of Earth except for the polar regions), a great
deal would s ll depend on the kind of star it was revolving about. Stars
that are much more massive than the sun, for instance, would not be
very apt to have such planets; their lives on the main sequence are too
short. A er all, here on Earth, organisms as advanced as primi ve
shellfish did not appear un l life had existed on the planet for 3 billion
years. If that is the normal rate of evolu on, then a planet circling a
star such as Sirius could never have life advanced beyond the simplest
form of bacterial life, for a er a mere half-billion years, Sirius would
become a red giant and destroy the planet.

Furthermore, if a star is very small and dim, a planet must be very
close to it to get enough light and heat to support life as we know it.
But at that close distance, dal effects would cause the planet to face
only one side to the sun, so that half the planet would be too hot and
half too cold.

In other words, we need stars about the size of our sun.
Then again, such stars cannot be part of close binaries or in other
regions where there would be too much energe c radia on from
surrounding stars. Suppose we decide that only one out of three
hundred stars has a chance of possessing a planet that would be
hospitable to our kind of life, and only one out of three hundred of
such stars has a planet of the right size, chemical composi on, and
temperature to actually support life. That might s ll mean the
existence of millions of life-bearing planets sca ered among the stars.

However, what are the chances that on one of these planets
intelligent life has developed, capable of developing a technology like
ours?

There are no op mis c answers to that ques on. A er all, Earth had
to exist for 4.6 billion years before a life-form appeared that was
capable of developing technology.

Even if the chances of its happening are small, it might s ll be that
thousands of technologies have developed among the stars, but then
there's a s ll more difficult ques on: How long would such
technologies endure?

Intelligent beings, as they learn to dispose of great sources of
energy, might use them for self-destruc ve purposes. Certainly, now
that mankind has developed advanced technologies, we have begun
to use them in devasta ng wars and are in the process of destroying
our environment with them. If this is typical, then the universe might
be full of life-bearing planets that have not yet achieved a technology,
and equally full of others that have already achieved an advanced
technology and have destroyed themselves. There would be only a
very, very few besides ourselves who had achieved the technology and
had not yet had me to destroy themselves.

In about 1950, the Italian-American physicist Enrico Fermi (1901?
1954) asked the ques on: Where are they? What he meant was, if the
stars are rich in technologies, why hasn't some alien life-form reached
us? (We can't count wild tales of flying saucers and ancient astronauts,
because the evidence in their favor is extremely weak.)
Perhaps aliens have not appeared because the distances between
the stars is too great to cross, or they have reached us and decided to
let us develop in peace, or have failed to appear for any number of
other reasons. We can't be sure that simply because no alien is here,
there are no aliens somewhere out there.

Some astronomers are eager to search for evidence of alien
civiliza ons, and we will return to them in due course.
90. WHAT ARE GLOBULAR CLUSTERS?

Stars don't necessarily exist singly, as our sun does. Herschel
discovered binary stars, and there is evidence that more than half the
stars in the sky belong to binary systems.

Stars also exist in more elaborate clusters. Even before the days of
the telescope, people admired the Pleiades, a group of stars in the
constella on Taurus. Pleiades includes six stars that can be seen by the
unaided eye, though some can see a seventh. When Galileo turned his
telescope on the Pleiades in 1610, he found he could count 36 stars,
and modern photographs show over 250.

Even the Pleiades represent only a small cluster. The turning point
where clusters were concerned came with Messier's list, which I
men oned earlier. The thirteenth item on this list, M13, was a fuzzy
object in the constella on Hercules. When William Herschel studied it
a couple of decades later with a much be er telescope than the one
Messier had used, he found that it was a huge cluster of closely
packed stars. It is now called the great Hercules cluster, and must
contain at least 100,000 stars. Because the cluster is shaped like a
globe, it is an example of what's known as a globular cluster. There are
about a hundred globular clusters now known.

The globular clusters are distributed with an odd asymmetry. The
Bri sh astronomer John Herschel (1792?1871) pointed out that they
were not distributed evenly over the sky; almost all of them were
located on one side of the sky, and virtually none on the other. In fact,
fully one third of the globular clusters were in the constella on
Sagi arius, which made up only 2 percent of the sky.

This observa on, as we shall soon see, turned out to be of great
importance.
91. WHAT ARE NEBULAS?

Not everything in the sky consists of stars or clusters of stars.
In 1694, Huygens saw and described a bright, fuzzy region in the

constella on Orion. It looked like a luminous cloud and came to be
called a nebula, which is simply the La n word for cloud. The nebula
described by Huygens is now known as the Orion nebula. We know it
to be a huge cloud of dust and gas about thirty light-years across. If
our en re solar system from the sun to the farthest comet were
placed within the Orion nebula, it would be lost in the cloud's
immensity, and even our sun and a dozen of its neighboring stars could
all fit comfortably within it. In fact, the Orion nebula has many stars
included within it, and it is those stars that cause it to shine by
reflected light.

In 1864, the Bri sh astronomer William Huggins (1824?1910)
managed to study the spectrum of the Orion nebula. It showed
individual bright lines against a dark background, as one would expect
of a hot gas, and the predica on that it was a huge cloud (of perhaps
the same type from which our solar system formed) was confirmed.
Indeed, the Orion nebula is one place where astronomers are
reasonably certain that new stars are being formed at this present

me. Many other glowing nebulae of widely different shapes, some of
remarkable beauty, have also been detected in the sky.

A nebula need not, however, necessarily be a glowing object. One
that did not happen to have any stars within it would be a dark nebula.
Herschel, for instance, spo ed small dark areas in otherwise crowded
starry regions, areas where no stars shine. He was puzzled by them
and thought they might represent tunnels of starlessness, so to speak,
the mouths of which just happened to be facing us. There were so
many of them, however, that that explana on seemed inadequate.
Surely there wouldn't be that many tunnels, all with their mouths
directly facing us.

About 1900, E. E. Barnard and, independently, the German
astronomer Max F. J. C. Wolf (1863?1932) suggested that the tunnels
were dark nebulas that obscured the light of the stars behind them.
Apparently, the starry skies were filled with clouds of dust that hid
some of the glory of the universe, a fact that turned out to have great
significance in our interpreta on of how the stars are distributed in
space.
92. WHAT IS THE GALAXY?

If we simply study the sky with our unaided eye, it would seem that
we see stars in every part of it. There are no areas that seem
par cularly full of stars and none that are bare of them. From this we
can conclude that the stars are distributed about us evenly in all
direc ons, and if the stars as a whole form a collec on that has a
shape, that shape must be a sphere. That makes sense, too, since all
sizable astronomical

Of course, what we see with our unaided eye are just 6,000 stars
that are, by and large, rather close to us. What happens if we use the
telescope? The answer is we see many, many more stars, but again,
they seem to be distributed evenly around the sky--except for the
Milky Way.

To the unaided eye, the Milky Way is a faint luminous band (which
today we can hardly see if we live in ci es, because the sky glows with
human-made illumina on). It has a faint milky appearance, and
indeed, one myth tells us that once, when Hera, the wife of Zeus, was
suckling an infant, some of her milk poured out into the sky and
formed this faint luminous band. The Greeks called it galaxias kyklos
(ring of milk), and the Romans called it via lactea (milky way), which is
where we get the English name.

But what really is the Milky Way? If we dismiss mythology, we can
begin with the Greek philosopher Democritus (c. 470?c. 380 B.C.), who
suggested in about 440 B.C. that it was actually made up of a vast
number of dim stars that could not be seen individually, but that all
together gave rise to a so luminosity. No one paid any a en on to
this view, but he happened to be perfectly right. This was proved when
Galileo turned his first telescope on the sky in 1609 and found that the
Milky Way contained myriads of stars.

How many are "myriads"? The first impression that people get
when they look at the night sky is that the stars are innumerable, that
they are too many to count. But as I have men oned a few mes
already, the total number of stars visible to the unaided eye is only
about 6,000. But through a telescope the number is much higher. Does
that mean they are innumerable, a er all?

The stars in the direc on of the Milky Way are exceedingly
numerous, but the stars in other direc ons are sparse in comparison,
which means that we must abandon the no on of the en re body of
stars forming a spherical structure. If that were so, the stars would be
as numerous in all direc ons, as in the Milky Way, and the en re sky
would be lit up luminously, with the nearer stars shining (less
spectacularly than they are now) against that faintly luminous
background.

We must suppose, then, that the stars exist in a large cluster that is
not spherical in shape, but extends out in the direc on of the Milky
Way for far greater distances than in any other. It might be, then, that
the Milky Way indicates that the stars are all clustered together in the
shape of a lens, or a hamburger pa y. This lens-shaped cluster is called
the Galaxy (from the Greek expression for the Milky Way), while Milky
Way is a name retained for the band of dim light we see encircling the
sky.

The first person to suggest that the stars exist in a fla ened galaxy
was an English astronomer, Thomas Wright (1711?1786). He made the
sugges on in 1750, but his ideas seemed so confused and mys cal
that few people paid any a en on to him at first.

Of course, even if the Galaxy was lens-shaped, it might stretch out
in the long diameter forever. There might be rela vely few stars if you
look away from the Milky Way, but an innumerable number in the
direc on of the Milky Way itself.

To se le the ma er, William Herschel undertook to count the stars.
Naturally, it was imprac cal to expect to count all the stars in any
reasonable length of me. What Herschel did was to choose 683 small
regions, well sca ered over the sky, and count the stars visible in his
telescope in each one. In this way, he took what we would now call a
"straw poll" of the heavens. This was the first case of applying sta s cs
to astronomy.

Herschel found that the number of stars in each region increased
steadily as he approached the Milky Way in any direc on. From the
number of stars he counted, he could make es mates as to the total
number of stars in the Galaxy and how large it might be. He
announced his results in 1785 and suggested that the long diameter of
the Galaxy was about 800 mes the distance between the sun and
Sirius, and the short diameter was 150 mes that distance.
Once the actual distance of Sirius was worked out, a half century
later, it could be seen that Herschel felt the Galaxy was 8,000 light-
years in its long diameter and 1,500 light-years in its short diameter.
He also calculated that it contained 800 million stars. This is a large
number, but not innumerable.

In the last two centuries, astronomers have probed the Galaxy with
far be er instruments than were available to Herschel and with far
be er techniques, and now it is known that the Galaxy is much larger
than Herschel had thought. The stretch of stars is at least 100,000
light-years in its long diameter, and might contain as many as 200
billion stars. Nevertheless, Herschel can be credited with having
discovered the Galaxy, so to speak, and having shown that the stars
are not innumerable, but can be counted.
93. WHERE IS THE CENTER OF THE GALAXY?

Since 1805, when Herschel found that the sun moved rela ve to the
nearby stars, it was understood that the sun was not the immovable
center of the universe. It nevertheless seemed to have a central or
nearly central posi on as far as the Galaxy was concerned.

As it happens, the Milky Way is almost evenly illuminated all around
the sky, which would make it seem reasonable to assume that the sun
is located near its center. If it was located to one side of the center,
then the Milky Way would seem much thicker and brighter in one
direc on than in the other. Looking away from the center toward the
nearby end of the Galaxy, we would see rela vely few stars. Toward
the center, on the other hand, we would be facing the faraway other
end of the Galaxy, where there would appear to be vast numbers of
stars.

Yet, despite how reasonable it seemed, this view that the sun must
be at or near the center of the Galaxy did not hold up. If it were so, not
only would all the stars of the Milky Way be evenly distributed about
us, but every other aspect of the Galaxy should be symmetric as well,
and this is not so. There is, a er all, the ma er of the globular clusters,
which I discussed a li le earlier. Almost all of them are on one side of
the heavens, and one third are in the single constella on of
Sagi arius.

Why that peculiar asymmetry? The answer began to come in 1912,
when the American astronomer Henrie a Swan Leavi (1868?1921)
was studying the Magellanic clouds. These are two hazy patches, the
Large Magellanic Cloud and the Small Magellanic Cloud, that look like
detached por ons of the Milky Way. They can be viewed only from the
Southern Hemisphere, and are named for the first European to see
them, Ferdinand Magellan, who discovered them as he was passing
through the Strait of Magellan in the far south of South America in
1521.

John Herschel had studied them from an observatory at the
southern p of Africa in 1834 and found that, like the Milky Way, they
were made up of vast assemblages of stars. The Magellanic clouds
stretched out over many light-years of space, but they are so far away
from us that all their stars can be considered to be at roughly the same
distance from Earth (in the same way that although people are spread
out all over the city of Chicago, all of them are at approximately the
same distance from Paris).

The Small Magellanic Cloud contains a number of Cepheid variables,
the type of variable star discovered by John Goodricke in 1784, all of
which, then, are located at about the same distance from us. Cepheid
variables come in varying degrees of brightness, a feature that is
determined by two factors: mass and distance. A er all, brightness
increases with the mass of a star and decreases with its distance from
us. An unusually bright Cepheid, therefore, must either be very
massive or very close to us, and it is ordinarily impossible to tell which
alterna ve is true. But since the Cepheid variables in the Small
Magellanic Cloud are all considered to be about the same distance
from us, distance can, in their case, be eliminated from considera on.
If we note that one Cepheid is brighter than another in that cloud, we
know that the brighter Cepheid is the more massive of the two and is
actually the more luminous.

Leavi found that in the Small Magellanic Cloud the brighter and
more luminous the Cepheid, the longer its period of varia on; there
was a smooth rela onship between the luminosity and the period.

Suppose, then, you found the distance of one par cular Cepheid
and measured its period. From these factors you would be able to
determine its luminosity and derive the luminosity-period curve that
Leavi had discovered.

You could then study any other Cepheid in the cloud. From its
period, you could find its luminosity by using Leavi 's curve, and from
that, you would be able to tell how far away it must be to appear as
bright as it does in the sky. In this way, the "Cepheid yards ck" could
be used to measure distances of stars that are far too distant to show
a measurable parallax.

One catch, though, was that even the nearest Cepheid was too far
away to determine its distance by parallax, so we didn't have the
distance figure that was necessary to set up the yards ck in the first
place.

In 1913, however, Ejnar Hertzsprung (who discovered red giants)
managed to work out, by means of a careful line of reasoning, the
distance of some Cepheids without depending on parallax. That set up
the yards ck.

In 1914, the American astronomer Harlow Shapley (1885?1972)
applied the yards ck to Cepheid variables he located in various
globular clusters. He found the distance to each and then designed a
model of each cluster at their respec ve direc ons and distances. This
gave him a three-dimensional model of all the globular clusters, which,
he discovered, formed a more or less spherical ball, with its center
thousands of light-years away in the direc on of Sagi arius.

Shapley thought it reasonable to suppose that the sphere of
globular clusters was at the center of the Galaxy, which thus seemed
to be far away from us. He overes mated that distance, actually, and
we now know that the sun is not located at or near the center of the
Galaxy, but 30,000 light-years to one side.

In that case, why don't we see the Milky Way as far, far brighter in
the direc on of Sagi arius than it is in the opposite direc on?
Actually, the Milky Way is, to some extent, brighter and more complex
in the Sagi arius direc on than in any other, but we can't see the
center of the Galaxy and beyond. The dark nebulas that li er the Milky
Way obscure the large majority of stars in that direc on.

What we see, then, when we look at the sky is only the rather small
por on of the Galaxy that comprises the outer region nearest our
solar system--our own neighborhood, in other words. If we consider
only this por on of the Galaxy, then, yes, we are near its center, but
we are nevertheless far from its actual center.
94. WHAT IS THE DOPPLER EFFECT?

In order to find out more about the Galaxy, we must study another
way of determining stars' mo ons. When Halley discovered that the
stars moved, he could only measure the way they moved across the
line of vision (the proper mo on) as if they were sliding along the
sphere of the sky. Once it became quite apparent, though, that the
sphere of the sky did not exist and that stars were distributed nearer
to us and farther from us through vast distances of space, the ques on
arose: Is a par cular star moving toward us or away from us? This
mo on, toward or away, is called radial mo on, because the star is
viewed as moving toward or away from us along the spoke (or radius)
of a wheel stretching away from us, with the Earth at the hub.

How can we possibly detect this mo on? If a star is moving directly
away from us or toward us, its posi on in the sky would not change.
Of course, if it moved away from us, it would appear dimmer and
dimmer in the sky, and if it moved steadily toward us, it would appear
brighter and brighter, but stars are so far away and move so slowly in
comparison to their mighty distances, that it could easily take
thousands of years for a star to change brightness enough to be
detected even with delicate instruments. Furthermore, even if a star
moved across the sky with a proper mo on, it might also be moving
toward or away from us so that it had a slan ng mo on in three
dimensions. How could such mo on possibly be seen?
The answer was found in a phenomenon observed on Earth that
seemed to have nothing to do with stars. Thus, it was no ced that if a
horseman was charging forward in a military a ack, blowing a trumpet
to hearten his troops and frighten the enemy, the trumpet seemed to
change pitch as the horseman passed a sta onary listener. At the
moment of passing, the sound suddenly took on a lower pitch.

This phenomenon might have gone unno ced in the heat of ba le,
but in 1815, the railroad locomo ve was invented by the Bri sh
engineer George Stephenson (1781?1848), and it was not many years
before such locomo ves were traveling as rapidly as a galloping horse,
or even more rapidly. What's more, they usually had whistles of some
sort to warn people when they passed through populated regions, so
it became very common to hear the sudden lowering of pitch as the
locomo ve passed, and the ques on of why it happened arose.

The Austrian physicist Chris an Johann Doppler (1803?1853)
tackled the problem and decided, quite correctly, that when a
locomo ve was approaching, each successive sound wave caught up
slightly with the one before it so they hit the ear more frequently than
they would have if the locomo ve had been sta onary. The pitch of
the whistle was higher, therefore, than it would have been if the
locomo ve were sta onary.

When the locomo ve passed a listener and began to recede, each
successive sound wave was pulled away from the one before it, and
they hit the ear less frequently than if the locomo ve had been
sta onary, so they sounded lower in pitch. As the locomo ve passed,
then, there was a natural change from higher than normal to lower
than normal, from a high pitch to a low pitch.
In 1842, Doppler worked out a mathema cal rela onship between
speed and pitch and tested it successfully by having a locomo ve pull
a flatcar back and forth at different speeds. On the flatcar were
trumpeters, sounding various notes. On the ground, musicians with a
sense of absolute pitch recorded the change as the train passed. Such
changes in pitch were therefore called the Doppler effect.

By this me, it had been discovered that light also consisted of
waves, though its waves were much, much nier than those of sound.
The French physicist Armand Hippolyte Fizeau (1819?1896) pointed
out in 1848 that the Doppler effect should work for any wave mo on,
including light. As a result, the way it works with light is some mes
called the Doppler-Fizeau effect.

If a star is neither approaching nor receding from us, the dark lines
in its spectrum should remain in place. If the star is moving away from
us, the light it emits is longer in wavelength (the equivalent of
deepening in pitch), and the dark lines all shi toward the red end of
the spectrum (a redshi ). The greater the shi , the more rapidly the
star is receding from us.

If, on the other hand, the star is approaching us, the light it emits is
shorter in wavelength (the equivalent of becoming higher in pitch),
and the spectral lines shi toward the violet end of the spectrum.
Again, the further the shi , the more rapidly the star is approaching.

If we know both the radial mo on (in or out) and the proper mo on
(to one side), we can calculate the true mo on of a star in three
dimensions. But, actually, the radial velocity is the much more
important of the two. The proper mo on can be measured only if a
star is close enough to make its mo on across the sky rapid enough to
be no ceable, and only a very ny frac on of all the stars are that
close to us. Radial mo on, on the other hand, can be determined, no
ma er how far away a star is, provided its spectrum can be obtained.

In 1868, William Huggins was the first to determine the radial
velocity of a star. He found that Sirius was moving away from us at
about 46 kilometers (29 miles) per second. We have be er figures
now, but that was quite close for a first a empt.
95. IS THE GALAXY ROTATING?

Every object we know of in the solar system is rota ng, from the sun
to the asteroids, though some objects rotate more rapidly than others.
This gives us the no on that other stars must also be rota ng and that,
in fact, even the en re Galaxy is rota ng. But how can we tell if this is
so?

Once astronomers were able to work out the true mo on, in three
dimensions, of a number of stars, it became possible to see that the
stars were not moving in different direc ons randomly.

In 1904, for instance, the Dutch astronomer Jacobus Cornelius
Kapteyn (1851?1922) found that a number of stars in the Big Dipper
and elsewhere in the sky, too, were all moving in the same direc on,
more or less. He found, in fact, that there were two streams of stars,
one moving with the stars of the Big Dipper and another moving in the
opposite direc on.

In 1927, J. H. Oort (who later advanced the theory of a distant cloud
of comets) interpreted the existence of the two star streams in the
following way. The stars of the Galaxy are all revolving about the
galac c center. Those nearer the center than our sun are moving faster
than the sun and are gaining on us, so that they all seem to be moving
in one direc on past us, with some, of course, moving more slowly
than others. Stars farther from the center than the sun are moving
more slowly, and the sun gains on them, so that they seem to be
moving in the opposite direc on from those that are nearer the
center. So if all the stars are moving in the same direc on, some faster,
some slower, about the galac c center, it is fair to say that the Galaxy
is rota ng.

This rota on gives rise to an important conclusion. Astronomers
have reason to believe that stars are closer together and exist in
greater and greater thickness as we approach the center of the Galaxy.
In fact, it seems likely that 90 percent of all the mass of the Galaxy is to
be found in a compara vely small volume at the center. The stars
outside the center circle that central mass, much as the planets of the
solar system circle the sun.

Oort, in determining that the Galaxy rotated, showed that the sun
revolved about the center of the Galaxy once in about 230 million
years. From this period of revolu on and from the distance of the sun
from the center, it is possible to calculate the mass of the central
conglomera on of stars.

It turns out that the mass of the Galaxy--or at least of the stars that
make it up--is about 100 billion mes that of the sun, which does not
mean that there are 100 billion stars in the Galaxy, for the sun is not a
representa ve star. Probably three quarters of the stars in the Galaxy
are red dwarfs, and fully ninety percent are less massive than the sun.
If the average star has half the mass of the sun, there might be
approximately 200 billion stars in the Galaxy.
96. DOES ANYTHING REACH US FROM THE STARS
BESIDES LIGHT?

Un l well into the 1900s, the only informa on of consequence that we
received from the starry universe outside our solar system was light. It
was by means of light that we studied the posi ons of stars, their
brightness, their mo ons, their temperatures, their chemical
composi on, and even their gravita onal effects on one another. But
there are other types of informa on reaching us from the stars.

A er radioac vity was discovered in 1896, scien sts learned to
detect radioac ve radia ons by means of various devices. Even small
quan es of such radia on were detectable. It was possible to shield
such detectors behind lead barriers that would absorb the energe c
radia ons, and from the thickness of lead required to block the
radia ons, the energy content could be es mated. What surprised
scien sts was that when enough shielding was supplied to block off all
radioac ve radia ons they knew about, some par cularly energe c
radia on s ll managed to get through to the detec ng device. The
ques on was, what was this unknown radia on?
It seemed to an Austrian-American physicist, Victor Franz Hess
(1883?1964), that whatever the radia on was, it had to come from
some earthly source. In 1911, he set about proving this by carrying a
detec ng device, well shielded, high into the air in balloons. He made
ten balloon ascensions, five at night, going as high as six miles. To his
astonishment, he found that the higher he went, the stronger the
radia on he detected. At the greatest heights he reached, the
radia on was eight mes as intense as it was on Earth's surface, from
which he could only conclude that the radia on was not coming up
from the Earth, but down from space. Others began to inves gate this
radia on that was showering the Earth from all direc ons, and in 1925
the American physicist Robert Andrews Millikan (1868?1953) named
the radia on cosmic rays, because they reached us from the cosmos.

The ques on remained as to the nature of cosmic rays. Millikan
himself thought they were light-like waveforms that were much more
energe c than light itself. Another American physicist, Arthur Holly
Compton (1892?1962), thought, instead, that they consisted of very
energe c electrically charged subatomic par cles, traveling nearly at
the speed of light. But how could this be proven?

If cosmic rays were light-like, they would be unaffected by Earth's
magne sm; if they were charged par cles, they would be bent by the
field and more would reach the Earth near the magne c poles than far
away from them. In the 1930s, Compton traveled over the world,
measuring the cosmic ray intensity here and there, and found that,
indeed, it was more intense as one approached the magne c poles.
Cosmic rays were charged par cles.

As it turned out, they were the same kind of charged par cles
emi ed by the sun as solar wind. They were posi vely charged atomic
nuclei, mostly hydrogen nuclei. When solar flares emit unusually
energe c solar wind jets, the speeding atomic nuclei can be so
energe c they are actually weak cosmic rays. The cosmic rays that
reach us from beyond the solar system, however, are far more
energe c than any that the sun can produce and are probably caused
by events that are far more energe c than a mere solar flare, like a
supernova explosion, for instance. Second, as cosmic rays pass among
the stars, they are con nually bent by the presence of magne c fields,
which tend to accelerate them and increase their energy.

Cosmic rays are important for many reasons. Earlier, I men oned
their influence on biological evolu on, but they also tell us something
about the chemical cons tu on of the universe generally and help
physicists by producing collisions with atoms in the atmosphere that
are far more energe c than any we can cause ar ficially even today.
(However, wai ng for cosmic ray collisions of the proper sort can be
very tedious, whereas we now have atom-smashing devices that can
create collisions that might be milder, but can be had on demand in
any quan ty.)

An important shortcoming of cosmic rays is that they cannot give us
specific informa on about specific events in the universe. Their paths
are so bent by magne c fields that there is no possible way of telling
from what direc on they originally came.

Aside from cosmic rays, there are also neutrinos, uncharged
subatomic par cles that have no mass and that therefore travel
through space at the speed of light. Their existence was first predicted,
on theore cal grounds, by the Austrian physicist Wolfgang Pauli
(1900?1958) in 1931. The par cle was named the neutrino (Italian for
li le neutral one) by Enrico Fermi in 1932.
Since neutrinos have no mass or electric charge and since they
interact with ordinary ma er only very rarely, they are almost
impossible to detect. Their existence is so convenient that physicists
accepted their reality, but it was not un l 1956 that the neutrino was
actually detected by two American physicists, Frederick Reines (b.
1918) and Clyde Lorrain Cowan (b. 1919).

Unlike cosmic rays, they aren't charged and are not affected by
magne c fields, but instead travel in lines as remorselessly straight
(except for the ny effects of gravita onal fields) as the path of light.
They are produced in huge quan es by the sun and by other stars,
and it is es mated that the universe contains a billion mes as many
neutrinos as the more ordinary kinds of subatomic par cles.

The catch is that neutrinos pass through ma er without colliding
with anything except at rare intervals, so that one can detect only a
few out of many trillions passing through detec on devices. For
decades, for instance, Reines has been trying to detect the neutrinos
given off by the sun. He has, indeed, con nually been detec ng some,
but only one third as many as are theore cally to be expected. The
reason for this is not known, and it is now referred to as "the mystery
of the missing neutrinos."

In 1987, when a supernova appeared in the Large Magellanic Cloud
(the closest supernova to us since 1604, for it was only 150,000 light-
years away), the first indica on that it had exploded was the sudden
appearance of seven detected neutrinos in a detec ng device (a
neutrino telescope) under the Alps mountain range. As detec ng
devices improve, addi onal neutrinos may be found that will tell us
more about energe c events in the universe. In any case, 1987 saw
the beginning of "neutrino astronomy" outside the solar system.
Another par cle that comes to us from space is the graviton.
Gravitons are uncharged, massless par cles that, like neutrinos, travel
at the speed of light. They are the least energe c par cles we know of
and the hardest to detect. Their existence was first predicted by Albert
Einstein in 1916, but all a empts to find them so far have failed,
though physicists are sure they exist. If they could be detected, they
would probably yield informa on about the most energe c events in
the universe.

Light and light-like radia on travels through space as waveforms
that exist in par cle-like units. (All waves have par cle aspects, and all
par cles have wave aspects.) In 1905, Einstein called the par cle
aspect of light a photon, from a Greek word for light. By far the
greatest por on of the informa on we receive from the universe even
today is through photons, but not only through photons of visible
light. There are photons of many other types, both less and more
energe c than those of visible light.
97. WHAT IS THE ELECTROMAGNETIC SPECTRUM?

When Einstein introduced the concept of the photon, it became clear
that the shorter the waves of a par cular kind of light, the more
energe c the photons. Therefore red light, which has the longest
waves in the spectrum, has the least energe c photons. The photons
of orange, yellow, green, and blue light have successively more energy,
and violet light has the most energe c photons. The ques on is,
though, whether photons of ordinary light are all the photons there
are.

The answer is no, and that informa on has actually been known for
nearly two hundred years, since 1800, when William Herschel
discovered that the spectrum extended beyond the visible red end.
You'll recall that he put a thermometer in different parts of the
spectrum to see what temperatures he would obtain, and he found
that the temperature was higher just beyond the red end of the
spectrum than it was anywhere in the spectrum itself, which indicated
some sort of invisible radia on beyond the red end of the spectrum.
This light was called infrared (meaning below the red) radia on, and I
have already men oned it in connec on with the greenhouse effect.

In 1801, the Bri sh physicist Thomas Young (1773?1829) finally
demonstrated that light consisted of ny waves rather than ny
par cles. In 1850, the Italian physicist Macedonio Melloni (1798?
1854) was able to show that infrared radia on had all the proper es
of ordinary light, except that its waves were longer and did not affect
the eye. Once photons were understood, it was seen that infrared
photons were individually less energe c than the photons of visible
light.

Radia on also existed beyond the violet end of the spectrum. In
1801, the German physicist Johann Wilhelm Ri er (1776?1810) was
tes ng the manner in which light caused the darkening of certain
silver compounds. He found that this darkening was accelerated as he
moved down the spectrum toward the violet end, but that it was
faster than ever when he moved beyond the violet. Apparently, there
was also ultraviolet (meaning beyond the violet) radia on, though it
could not be seen, and we now know that ultraviolet light has shorter
waves than visible light and that its photons are more energe c.

About 1870, James Clerk Maxwell produced four equa ons that
described all the behavior of electricity and magne sm and showed
that these two phenomena were different aspects of a single
electromagne c interac on. What's more, he showed that if the
electromagne c field vibrated, it produced a waveform that moved at
the speed of light. If the vibra on was of the proper speed, light itself
was created, so that light could be considered an example of
electromagne c radia on.

At various speeds, however, the vibra on could produce longer and
longer waves, not only those of infrared radia on, but other forms of
radia on far beyond it, and shorter and shorter waves, including those
of ultraviolet radia on and beyond. In other words, there is an
electromagne c spectrum, with waves extending from the incredibly
short to the incredibly long, of which visible light comprises only a ny
stretch.
Once Maxwell pointed out that such extreme radia ons existed,
scien sts knew what to look for and were able to find them. In 1888, a
German physicist, Heinrich Rudolf Hertz (1857?1894), discovered what
came to be called radio waves, with wavelengths far longer than those
of infrared radia on. In 1895, another German physicist, Wilhelm
Conrad Roentgen (1845?1923), discovered X rays, which had
wavelengths far shorter than ultraviolet radia on. Then in 1900, the
French physicist Paul Ulrich Villard (1860?1934) discovered that
among the radia ons emi ed by radioac ve substances were gamma
rays, an electromagne c radia on with wavelengths shorter even than
X rays.

Radio-wave photons were less energe c than infrared photons; X-
ray photons were more energe c than ultraviolet photons; and
gamma-ray photons were more energe c s ll.

Stars tend to radiate photons through the en re range of
electromagne c radia on. In that case, why is it we are sensi ve to
only the ny range of the spectrum represented by visible light?

In the first place, a star like our sun delivers its peak intensity of
radia on in the visible light region, so it makes sense for lifeforms that
depend on the sun to develop sensory equipment that receives and
reacts to that range. Cooler stars, like red dwarfs, are much richer in
the less energe c infrared radia on. Ho er stars, like the massive
blue-whites, have many more of the energe c ultraviolet waves. Very
energe c events in these hot stars can produce unusually high bursts
of X rays and even gamma rays.

Second, Earth's atmosphere, while quite transparent to visible light,
is rela vely opaque to other por ons of the electromagne c
spectrum, so we don't have much chance to become aware of other
forms of light. But some of the infrared and ultraviolet waves near the
visible spectrum do manage to pass through; for example, the
ultraviolet light that penetrates the atmosphere is more energe c than
visible light and much more effec ve in producing sunburn.

Beginning in the 1950s, human beings began to send rockets
beyond the atmosphere, and equipped satellites placed into orbit
around the Earth with devices capable of recording por ons of the
electromagne c spectrum that could not penetrate Earth's
atmosphere. By studying X rays emi ed by the sun's corona, for
instance, astronomers could prove that its temperature was a million
degrees. By studying the infrared radia ons, astronomers found bands
of dust about the bright star Vega that might indicate the existence of
planetary bodies, and conducted a search for brown dwarfs.
Ultraviolet emissions and even occasional bursts of gamma rays are
also being studied.

There is no ques on, however, that in astronomy the most useful
por on of the electromagne c spectrum has come to be the region of
the radio waves.
98. HOW DID RADIO ASTRONOMY DEVELOP?

In 1931, the American radio engineer Karl Guthe Jansky (1905?1950),
working for Bell Telephone Laboratories, was tackling the problem of
sta c that interfered with ship-to-shore telephone communica ons
using radio waves. Sta c has a number of causes, including
thunderstorms, nearby electric equipment, and aircra passing
overhead, all of which produce radio waves that interfere with the
orderly radio waves used in telephone communica on by introducing
random effects that cause random sound--the crackling noise of
sta c.

Jansky built a device that was capable of detec ng these
bothersome interfering radio waves, and while using it, he detected a
new kind of weak sta c, a kind of hiss, whose source he could not
iden fy at first. It came from overhead and moved steadily from day to
day. At first it seemed to Jansky that it moved with the sun. However,
it gained slightly on the sun to the extent of four minutes a day--just
the amount by which the stars gain on the sun.

The source, Jansky therefore reasoned, must lie beyond the solar
system, and by 1932 he had decided that it came from the direc on of
the constella on Sagi arius, the direc on in which it was now known
the center of the Galaxy was located. Bell published his results before
the end of 1932, and though they a racted li le a en on at the me,
they marked the birth of radio astronomy.
One of the reasons Jansky's work drew so li le no ce was that
astronomers of the me, never suspec ng they might have to detect
radio waves from the sky, lacked the proper equipment to receive and
analyze such radia on. An American radio engineer, Grote Reber (b.
1911), tried to capitalize on Jansky's work, and in 1937, he built a
radio-wave receiving device in the shape of a paraboloid (the shape of
the casing that holds an automobile headlight). Thirty-one feet in
diameter, it received and reflected radio waves, focusing them to the
point where they could be more easily studied. In this way, he
constructed the first radio telescope and became the first radio
astronomer.

Reber discovered and mapped regions in the sky that seemed to be
stronger-than-usual sources of radio waves. He called these radio
stars, and the whole was a radio map. He published his results in
1942, but World War II was raging, and people paid li le a en on.

Nevertheless, the shortest radio waves, called microwaves, lying just
next to the infrared por on of the spectrum, turned out to be very
useful in the war. Such microwaves could be sent out in impulses that
would be reflected by aircra . From the direc on in which the
reflec ons came and from the me lapse between pulse and
reflec on, one could calculate where a plane was, in what direc on it
was flying, and how fast. This was called radio detec on and ranging,
where ranging means distance determina on. The phrase was
abbreviated to radar.

The development of radar moved quickly in Great Britain under the
guidance of the Bri sh physicist Robert Alexander Watson-Wa
(1892?1973). It was radar, more than anything else, that enabled the
numerically inferior Bri sh Royal Air Force to defeat the German
Lu waffe in the Ba le of Britain in late 1940.

In the process of developing radar, devices were invented that could
detect radio waves, and once the war was over, it became possible to
detect those coming from space with great precision. Larger and larger
radio telescopes were built, for large radio telescopes could be built
much more easily than equally large op cal telescopes.

Of course, microwaves are much longer than waves of visible light,
which meant one saw much more "fuzzily" by microwaves than by
visible light. As radio telescopes became larger, however, microwave
vision improved. Indeed, large radio telescopes could be built very far
apart and could be synchronized by computers, so that the effect was
of a radio telescope many miles in diameter. Microwave vision then
became far sharper than light vision.

Since the 1950s, therefore, radio astronomy has been extremely
useful in giving us informa on about the universe that ordinary light
astronomy could not possibly have given us. As a result, we have
learned more about the universe in the last thirty years than in all the

me before.
99. WHAT ARE PULSARS?

Once astronomers zeroed in on microwave detec on, they found it
had two enormous advantages. First, it was the only important region
of the en re electromagne c spectrum, other than visible light, to
which Earth's atmosphere was transparent. There was a microwave
window to the outer universe, as well as a light window, which meant
that the microwave radia ons of the universe could be studied from
Earth's surface and it was not necessary to use rockets.

Second, microwaves could penetrate fog, mist, and dust clouds that
were opaque to ordinary light. This was discovered in connec on with
radar during the war, for incoming planes could be followed even
when they thought themselves hidden by fog or clouds. Similarly,
por ons of the outer universe that were opaque to visible light were
transparent to microwaves, and we could study with microwaves what
we could not see with light. Thus, the center of the Galaxy, forever
hidden to our sight by dust clouds, could finally be studied through its
microwave emissions.

Closer to home, it was the microwave emission of Venus, first
detected in 1956, that gave astronomers their ini al hint that the
planet was extremely hot. What's more, rocket probes to Venus could
send out microwave beams that penetrated the cloud layer and were
reflected by the solid ground below. From these reflec ons, Venus'
surface could be mapped, beginning in 1962, although it could never
have been seen by visible light, except for the small patches that could
be photographed by cameras dropped into the atmosphere.

Radar reflec ons could also be used to determine the rota on rates
of Venus and Mercury. It was found that Venus rotated far more slowly
than had been thought (and in the wrong direc on), while Mercury
rotated far more rapidly.

In 1955, the American astronomer Kenneth Linn Franklin (b. 1923)
found a large emission of microwaves by Jupiter, which was finally
explained in 1960, when it was shown that Jupiter had an enormous
magne c field, much larger than Earth's. This was confirmed in the
1970s, when probes were sent out beyond Jupiter.

More spectacular discoveries were made through radio astronomy
in the universe beyond the solar system. As I men oned earlier, Zwicky
and Oppenheimer had independently speculated on the existence of
neutron stars, extremely condensed stars consis ng only of neutrons,
which squeezed the mass of an ordinary star into a ny ball just a few
kilometers across. The possibility that such a neutron star had formed
a er the supernova explosion responsible for the Crab nebula was
explored by the American astronomer Herbert Friedman (b. 1916). X
rays were detected from various parts of the sky, and one source was
the Crab nebula. Could the source be a neutron star remnant within
the Crab nebula?

In July 1964, the moon was to pass in front of the Crab nebula, and
Friedman supervised the sending of a rocket into space to monitor the
X-ray produc on during the event. If the X rays were coming from a
neutron star, then the X-ray emission would be cut off en rely and at
once as the moon passed in front of the ny object. If the X-ray
emission dropped off gradually as the moon moved in front of the
Crab nebula, then the source was the en re nebula and not a ny
object within it. The la er proved to be the case, and those who had
hoped to detect a neutron star in this way were disappointed.

In 1964, however, a new discovery was made. Radio waves from
certain regions in the sky seemed to show a rapid fluctua on in
intensity. It was as if there were "radio twinkles" here and there. The
Bri sh astronomer Antony Hewish (b. 1924) designed a radio
telescope that would make it possible to study fast changes in
microwave intensi es in greater detail. He supervised the construc on
of 2,048 separate receiving devices spread out over nearly three acres,
and in July 1967, they were put to work.

Within a month, a young Bri sh graduate student, Jocelyn Bell,
detected bursts of microwaves from a place midway between Vega
and Altair. The bursts were, as it happened, astonishingly brief, las ng
only one thir eth of a second. Even more astonishing, the bursts
followed one another with remarkable regularity. They were so
regular, in fact, that the period could be worked out to one hundred-
millionth of a second; it was 1.33730109 seconds. By February 1968,
when Hewish announced the discovery, he had located three other
such radio twinkles, and since then hundreds more have been
discovered.

Naturally, there was no way of telling, at first, what such a pulse
represented. Hewish could only think of it as a pulsa ng star, with
each pulsa on giving out a burst of energy. This name was shortened
almost at once to pulsar, by which the new objects came to be known.

All the pulsars are characterized by extremely regular radio pulses,
but the exact period varies from pulsar to pulsar. One had a period as
long as 3.7 seconds. In November 1968, astronomers detected a pulsar
in the Crab nebula that had a period of only 0.033089 seconds; it was
pulsing thirty mes a second. Since then a few pulsars have been
discovered that pulse several hundred mes a second.

The ques on was, what can produce such short flashes with such
fantas c regularity? Some object must be revolving, or rota ng, or
pulsing, and with each revolu on, rota on, or pulsa on, it must send
out a microwave burst. To do this, however, it must revolve, rotate, or
pulsate in a ma er of seconds, or even hundredths of a second, which
would require a very small size, combined with a very strong
gravita onal field. Pulsars can't be white dwarfs, for instance, because
white dwarfs are too large and their gravita onal fields are too weak.
If they could be imagined as forced to revolve, rotate, or pulsate with
sufficient speed, they would tear themselves apart.

The Austrian-American astronomer Thomas Gold (b. 1920)
suggested almost at once that a pulsar had to be a rota ng neutron
star. A neutron star was small enough to rotate in a ma er of a
frac on of a second and would have a surface gravity intense enough
to hold together when it did so. It had already been theorized that a
neutron star would have an enormously intense magne c field, with
magne c poles that need not be at the pole of rota on; electrons
would be held so ghtly by the gravita onal pull of the neutron star
that they would be able to escape only at the magne c poles. As the
electrons were thrown off, they would lose energy in the form of
microwaves. If, as the neutron star rotates, the microwaves happen to
be thrown off in our direc on, we get one burst, or possibly two, for
every rota on.

Gold pointed out that as the microwaves were given off, the
neutron star would lose rota onal energy and its period would very
slowly increase. This hypothesis was tested for various pulsars and the
increase was found. In par cular, the period of the Crab nebula pulsar
was slowing by 36.48 billionths of a second each day.

The Crab nebula thus had a neutron star inside it, a er all. But other
parts of the Crab nebula also emi ed X rays. Only 5 percent of the X
rays come from the pulsar, which was what misled Friedman. In 1969,
astronomers found that the Crab nebula pulsar also emi ed very brief
flashes of light with each revolu on. It flicked on and off 30 mes a
second, so it was called an op cal pulsar.

The first really fast pulsar was located in 1982. It sent out radio
pulses 642 mes a second. It is probably smaller than most pulsars,
perhaps not more than 5 kilometers (3 miles) in diameter and with a
mass perhaps two or three mes that of our sun. Other fast pulsars
have also been located.

Some mes a pulsar will suddenly speed up its period very slightly,
then resume the slowing trend. Some astronomers suspect that such a
glitch might be the result of a starquake, a shi ing of mass distribu on
within the neutron star. Or it might be caused by a sizable body
plunging into the neutron star and adding its own momentum to the
star's.
100. WHAT ARE BLACK HOLES?

Back in about 1800, Laplace (who first advanced the nebular
hypothesis) pointed out that the more massive and dense an object
was, the higher its surface gravity and the higher its escape velocity.
There were combina ons of mass and density that would produce so
high a surface gravity that the escape velocity would equal or surpass
the speed of light. In that case, light could not be emi ed by the
object.

At the me, the hypothesis just seemed an idle specula on, for
nothing was known that was nearly massive enough or dense enough
(one or the other or both) to bring about such a situa on.

In 1939, however, when Oppenheimer worked out the proper es of
a neutron star, he pointed out that if such a neutron star was more
than 3.2 mes the mass of the sun, even the neutrons of which it was
composed would not be able to resist the gravita onal pull inward.
The neutrons would collapse, and there would be nothing else strong
enough to resist gravity, which would therefore bring about a total
collapse to a singularity, a point of virtually no volume and virtually
infinite mass and density.

Such a super neutron star would not be able to give off light, as
Laplace had predicted. It could be pictured as an infinitely deep "hole"
in space, into which anything could fall, but nothing could get out.
Since not even light could escape, the American physicist John
Archibald Wheeler (b. 1911) suggested it be called a black hole, and
the name stuck.

In 1970, however, the Bri sh physicist Stephen William Hawking (b.
1942) pointed out that black holes could very slowly "evaporate," so
they were not absolutely permanent objects.

Black holes are most likely to form where stars are strewn most
thickly, where stellar collisions are most frequent and stars might cling
together to form huge masses that can collapse. It follows that black
holes might be found at the center of globular clusters and are even
more likely to occur at the center of the Galaxy.

Indeed, the small core of our galaxy in the direc on of Sagi arius is
so ac ve--that is, it liberates so much energy (remember, Jansky's first
discovery of radio sources outside our solar system was from this core)
--that most astronomers are reasonably certain there is a black hole
at the center, with a mass of perhaps a hundred million stars.

Such a massive black hole would con nue to grow as it swallowed
ma er in the neighborhood, perhaps even en re stars, gulping them
down whole, so to speak. There is no danger, however, of the en re
Galaxy being swallowed down in the near future. As a black hole clears
the area around itself, there is less and less likelihood of further
acquisi ons.

The problem is, how does one actually observe a black hole to
determine whether it really exists? Since it emits no photons of any
kind, we can't see it anywhere along the electromagne c spectrum.
However, material trapped by the black hole's gravita onal field
revolves about it rapidly and, through collisions, loses energy and
tends to spiral into the black hole. The process of spiraling produces X
rays, so that wherever X rays are given off in the sky, we might at least
suspect the possibility of the existence of a black hole. Unfortunately,
there are other processes that might also release X rays, so that alone
only raises the possibility of a black hole and nothing more. Thus, even
if we know the center of the Galaxy is ac ve and producing a great
deal of radia on, that doesn't give us direct evidence of a black hole.

Suppose, though, a black hole is part of a close binary system, with
a normal star as its companion. A close binary system consis ng of a
white dwarf and a normal star can give rise to novas. Close binaries of
two neutron stars are also known to exist, and the study of their
mo on has been used to back up Einstein's general theory of rela vity.
Why not, then, a close binary system consis ng of a black hole and a
normal star?

If such a thing existed, ma er would be drawn from the normal star
into the black hole and would produce X rays as it spiraled down. Since
ma er would be drawn irregularly, the X rays would vary in quan ty
and intensity in an irregular fashion.

In 1965, a par cularly intense X-ray source was detected in the
constella on Cygnus and was named Cygnus X-1. In 1971, an X-ray-
detec ng rocket showed that the X rays emi ed by Cygnus X-1 were
irregular, indica ng the possibility of a black hole.

Cygnus X-1 was immediately inves gated with great care and was
found to exist in the immediate neighborhood of a large, hot blue-
white star es mated to be about thirty mes as massive as our sun.
This star and the X-ray source revolved about each other, and judging
from the loca on of the center of gravity, the X-ray source seemed to
be from five to eight mes as massive as our sun. Since it could not be
seen, it had to be a condensed star of ny size, and since it was too
massive to be a neutron star, it must be a black hole.
This is the nearest we've come to the actual detec on of a black
hole, and most astronomers accept Cygnus X-1 as such and are certain
that black holes exist and might even be fairly common.
101. WHAT IS IN INTERSTELLAR DUST CLOUDS?

There are clouds of dust and gas in interstellar regions--the space
between the stars. Scien sts were ini ally quite sure that the dust
consisted of fine grains of rocky materials and metals, the stuff that
ended up making up the smaller worlds when such clouds condensed
into stellar systems of stars and planets. As for the gas, that consisted
mostly of hydrogen and helium.

Even though the dust and gas are thick enough to obscure the stars
within and behind them, and plen ful enough to form stars and
planets, this material is spread over such vast spaces that at first
scien sts were confident that the dust par cles were ny and the
gases consisted of single atoms. They were just too widespread to
stand much chance of striking one another and coalescing.

Informa on about the actual content of the clouds was first
obtained by the German astronomer Johannes Franz Hartmann (1865?
1936) in 1904. He studied the radial velocity of the star Delta Orionis
and found that the various spectral lines shi ed by the same quan ty,
as was to be expected, though with some excep ons. The lines that
represented the element calcium did not budge. It did not seem likely
that the star would be moving and leaving calcium behind, and
Hartmann felt that he was detec ng calcium in the thin, largely
mo onless interstellar ma er that lay between the star and ourselves.

Of course, the major component of the interstellar ma er was
hydrogen, and beginning in 1951, spectral lines represen ng ionized
hydrogen (that is, hydrogen hot enough to lose the electrons from its
atoms) were detected by the American astronomer William Wilson
Morgan (b. 1906). The hydrogen was hot because of the near presence
of large blue-white stars, which apparently existed in curved lines in
the Galaxy. The hot hydrogen marked out these lines, so that the
structure of our galaxy could be viewed not as a simple lens shape, but
more as a pinwheel with spiral arms extending from the central
regions. Our solar system is in one of those arms.

Very li le could be seen in the interstellar clouds if only visible light
spectra were considered. With the coming of radio astronomy,
everything changed, for cold atoms and atom combina ons that
emi ed no light to speak of emi ed far more of the less energe c
microwaves.

In 1944, for instance, the Dutch astronomer Hendrik Christoffell Van
de Hulst (b. 1918), in hiding during the German occupa on of the
Netherlands during World War II and unable to work on astronomy in
the usual way, instead calculated how cold hydrogen atoms in space
might behave. He realized that these hydrogen atoms could each line
up their nucleus and their electron (they had only one electron apiece)
either in the same direc on or in opposite direc ons, and every once
in a while, a hydrogen atom shi ed from one configura on to the
other, emi ng a microwave with waves that were 21 cen meters (8.2
inches) long. Any given hydrogen atom would do this only every 11
million years or so, but there were so many hydrogen atoms in space
that some were always producing those microwaves. In 1951, the
American physicist Edward Mills Purcell (b. 1912) detected this
microwave emission, and therea er, it could be used to track unusual
concentra ons of cold hydrogen in interstellar space.

As methods for detec ng microwaves improved, minor components
of the gas clouds could be detected. For instance, there is a rare type
of hydrogen atom in which the nucleus is twice as massive as in
ordinary hydrogen atoms. Ordinary hydrogen is hydrogen 1, but the
more massive type is deuterium (from a Greek word for second), or
hydrogen 2. In 1966, microwaves were detected that were
characteris c of hydrogen 2, and there were some indica ons that in
the universe as a whole, 20 percent of all the hydrogen is in the form
of hydrogen 2.

Atom combina ons can be iden fied by their characteris c
microwave emissions. For instance, next to hydrogen, the most
common atoms in space that are capable of combining with other
atoms are oxygen atoms. It is not surprising that once in a long me,
an oxygen and a hydrogen atom might strike each other and cling
together in a combina on known as a hydroxyl group. Such a group
would emit or absorb microwaves in four characteris c wavelengths,
and two of them were observed in clouds in 1963.

Astronomers began to accept two-atom combina ons in the thin
interstellar ma er, though combina ons of three or more atoms s ll
seemed improbable. Yet toward the end of 1968, the microwave
fingerprints for water molecules (two hydrogen atoms and an oxygen
atom, three altogether) and ammonia molecules (three hydrogen
atoms and a nitrogen atom, four altogether) were also detected.
A er that, numerous rather complex combina ons were found,
invariably containing one or more carbon atoms, and the science of
astrochemistry was founded. Astronomers are s ll not quite certain
how these complex molecules, some made up of as many as thirteen
atoms, manage to be formed in the thin near-vacuum ma er of space,
but there is also the possibility that if we could send detec ng devices
into clouds of interstellar ma er (which we can't so far, for they are
many light-years away), we could detect s ll more complex groupings.
102. WHAT IS SETI?

Earlier, we speculated on the possibility of life on planets circling other
stars. We have not yet mastered a technology that would make it
possible for us to visit these planets, and aliens have not, to our
knowledge, visited us. So it is much more prac cal to send a message
than to send ships and intelligent beings in either direc on. Messages
do not involve the enormous expense of large rocket-powered
starships, nor do they endanger anyone's life. Moreover, whereas
starships might easily take centuries or millennia to reach even the
nearest stars, messages can move at the speed of light (which, by the
way, is the fastest possible speed, as Einstein showed in 1905) and
therefore take only years or decades.

As it happens, our civiliza on is not sufficiently advanced to send a
message by any means powerful enough to reach the distant stars
with reasonable intensity. We can assume, however, that if there are
alien intelligences out there, they might well be more advanced than
we are, so that our role would be to try to detect the messages of
others rather than to send out any of our own.

The ques on is, in what form are those messages likely to arrive?
They aren't likely to be modulated cosmic rays, since these are
wastefully energe c and follow curved paths, and therefore would be
sca ered and distorted without necessarily giving any indica on of
their point of origin. Neutrinos and gravitons would be too difficult to
detect. That leaves us with photons.
As for photons, we first have to rule out beams of light, which
wouldn't show up clearly amid the vast quan es of light produced by
the stars, and any photons more energe c than those of visible light
would be too wasteful. This leads us to assume that microwave
photons are the most likely means by which messages can be sent.

The search for extraterrestrial intelligence, which is abbreviated to
SETI, amounts, therefore, to carefully watching the sky for the
existence of any radio signals that are not absolutely regular, as are
pulsar bursts, or absolutely irregular, as are those arising from
turbulent clouds. Neither of these types of signals would indicate the
presence of an extraterrestrial intelligence. We would need signals
that are irregular, but clearly not randomly irregular.

There have been searches of this kind since the 1960s that have
been brief and limited and have detected nothing. What is really
needed is an elaborate system of detec on devices that can examine
the en re sky in detail over a considerable period of me.
Unfortunately, this would cost a great deal of money and labor, and
thus far mankind, although willing to spend trillions of dollars a year
on war and prepara ons for war, is not willing to spend far less for
something like SETI. The feeling is that it is not likely to succeed and
would just be money thrown away. There is even some suspicion,
among those who have seen too many primi ve mo on pictures, that
if we do anything to a ract the a en on of aliens, they might be
encouraged to come to Earth and conquer us (as though any aliens
could do more harm to us than we are busily engaged in doing to
ourselves).

Actually, SETI could prove to be a profitable procedure, even if we
found no messages. First, the a empts to put together devices that
would be suitable for receiving such messages would undoubtedly
succeed in improving our techniques for radio astronomy, and this
would be useful in many direc ons, even if we ended up making no
search.

Second, if we inspected the sky carefully and came across no
messages, we would be sure to find many objects of interest that we
would not have found without our new techniques and without a
careful, dedicated search. Pulsars, for instance, were not found
because anyone was looking for them. The discovery was
serendipitous, an unexpected by-product of a scien fic search.

Third, even if we detected some sort of message and could make
nothing out of it (and it is very likely we would not be able to interpret
the product of alien minds), the mere fact of its existence would prove
that it is possible for intelligent beings to a ain technological exper se
well beyond our own without necessarily destroying themselves.

Fourth, if the aliens were interested in having us understand them,
and if they deliberately made the message elementary enough for us
to interpret, the way might be opened for us to learn a great deal and
to advance our own knowledge far beyond the levels it might
ordinarily reach in a given me.
103. IS THE GALAXY THE ENTIRE UNIVERSE?

Once Herschel had demonstrated that the stars formed a lens-shaped
galaxy, it was assumed that that was the universe. And as its size came
to be understood--100,000 light-years across and perhaps 2 billion
stars--it certainly seemed large enough to represent a respectable
universe. No astronomer before the 1910s had dreamed of a universe
anywhere near that size.

And yet the Galaxy wasn't quite all there was to the universe. The
Magellanic clouds, in which Leavi had studied Cepheid variables and
worked out the yards ck that could be used to demonstrate the true
size of the Galaxy, lay outside the Galaxy. From their Cepheids, it could
be shown that the Large Magellanic Cloud was 160,000 light-years
away and the Small Magellanic Cloud was about 200,000 light-years
away. Of course, the Magellanic clouds could be looked on as satellites
of the Galaxy, as the moon is a satellite of the Earth and the planets
are satellites of the sun. In other words, we can consider the
Magellanic clouds as outlying districts, or suburbs, of the Galaxy.

Is there anything else that could possibly lie outside the Galaxy?
Suspicion, though not a very strong one, rested on the Andromeda
nebula (which we've men oned earlier in connec on with Laplace's
nebular hypothesis, for instance). The Andromeda nebula is visible as a
small object of the fourth magnitude that looks like a faint, slightly
fuzzy star to the unaided eye. The German astronomer Simon Marius
(1573?1624) was the first, in 1612, to view it through a telescope.
Messier included it in his list of fuzzy objects that were not comets. It
was the thirty-first on his list, so the Andromeda nebula is some mes
called M31.

Laplace worked out his nebular hypothesis under the inspira on of
the Andromeda nebula, which he thought looked like a mass of
swirling gas, so that it might be a star and planetary system in the
process of forma on. Immanuel Kant, who in 1755 had preceded
Laplace in thinking of such a hypothesis, had a different idea. He
thought that objects like the Andromeda nebula were immensely
distant systems of stars, and he called them island universes. He was
quite right, as it happened, but his idea was ignored.

The Andromeda nebula did have a certain whirling look about it,
and between 1845 and 1850, Lord Rosse (the first to give the Crab
nebula its name) observed over a dozen other nebulas that had the
same whirling look. In fact, some looked like pinwheels or whirlpools.
One object on Messier's list, M51, was so spectacularly whirlpool-like
in appearance that it was called the Whirlpool nebula.

These whirling nebulas were called spiral nebulas, and the
Andromeda nebula was one of them, but it was viewed so nearly
edge-on that the spiral nature was not easily seen. By the year 1900,
some 13,000 spiral neublas had been sighted, and it might be argued
that all of them were objects in the Galaxy, represen ng planetary
systems in the process of forma on. (Later, it was found that the
Galaxy itself had a spiral structure, but this was not known in 1900.)

By that me, though, light spectra of astronomical objects were
being studied. In 1864, William Huggins had taken the spectrum of the
Orion nebula and showed it to consist of bright lines against a dark
background, exactly what was to be expected from a mass of hot gas.

On the other hand, the spectrum of the Andromeda nebula, first
obtained in 1899, showed the kind of spectrum one would expect
from a star. Could it be, then, that the Andromeda nebula was a mass
of stars, but so much farther away than the Milky Way or the
Magellanic clouds that it was impossible to make out individual stars in
it? If so, it must lie far beyond our galaxy, and so must other spiral
nebulas, so that the universe might be enormously larger than our
galaxy alone.

How could the ma er be se led? If normal stars are just too far
away to be seen in the Andromeda nebula (assuming it is indeed made
up of stars), what about stars that are much brighter than ordinary
ones? What about novas?

As a ma er of fact, a nova did appear in the Andromeda nebula in
1885, and it was named S Andromedae. It became so bright that it was
almost visible to the unaided eye. However, there was no telling
whether it was really part of the Andromeda nebula or just a nova that
had developed in the direc on of the Andromeda nebula and was
shining in front of it, but had nothing to do with it.

The thing to do was to look for more novas, and an American
astronomer, Heber Doust Cur s (1872?1942), did just that. By careful
observa on, he picked up the ny sparkles of numerous novas in the
Andromeda nebula. There were so many of these novas that there was
no chance at all that they just happened to appear in different places
in the direc on of the nebula. No other equal-sized region of the sky
produced so many novas in so brief a me. Therefore the novas that
look as if they are in the Andromeda nebula really are there.

Second, most of the Andromeda novas were so faint they could
barely be seen. They were much fainter than the novas that were
indisputably a part of the Galaxy. The faintness of the Andromeda
novas made it seem as if the nebula must be very far away--and far
outside our galaxy. (Why, then, was S Andromedae so bright? Because,
astronomers later decided, it had not been an ordinary nova, but a
supernova.)

Cur s's no ons, expressed in 1918, shook the astronomic world,
which was reluctant to go along with him. In 1920, Cur s and Shapley
(who had recently determined the size of the Galaxy) debated the
ma er, with Shapley sternly opposed to Cur s's no ons. The debate
ended in a kind of draw, but as me went on, it became more and
more obvious that Cur s must be right.

The ma er was finally se led by Hubble, who made use of a new
hundred-inch telescope at Mt. Wilson Observatory in California. With
it, he could make out individual stars in the outskirts of the nebula,
which thus proved it to be a collec on of stars and not just a mass of
gas and dust. In 1923, he was able to iden fy one of the stars as a
Cepheid and to use it to es mate the distance of the Andromeda
nebula. His ini al figure was too low, but it was high enough to show
that the Andromeda nebula lies far outside our galaxy.

Nowadays, we know that the Andromeda nebula is 2.2 million light-
years away from us, a distance equal to 22 mes the total width of our
galaxy. With that, the Andromeda became known as the Andromeda
Galaxy, and the spiral nebulas as spiral galaxies.

So it seems that the universe consists of millions of galaxies,
perhaps billions, and is enormously larger than the Galaxy alone.
104. ARE THE GALAXIES MOVING?

Since it was se led four hundred years ago that the Earth is moving
about the sun, and one hundred and fi y years ago that the sun is
moving about the center of the Galaxy, it should come as no surprise if
we learned that the Galaxy is moving also.

A close study of the nearest galaxies shows that our own galaxy, the
Milky Way, is part of a cluster of galaxies called the local group. The
two chief members are the Milky Way and the Andromeda galaxy, the
Andromeda being even larger than our own, with at least 300 billion
stars. On the outskirts of the group is another large galaxy called the
Maffei 1 galaxy (a er the astronomer who first studied it), which may
or may not be part of the local group. In addi on, there are nearly
twenty smaller galaxies, each containing 100 billion stars or less.

The galaxies within the local group, including our own, move
majes cally about the center of gravity of the en re system, and they
can all be included in a sphere about 3.5 million light-years in
diameter. Yet even so vast a sphere represents only our immediate
neighborhood. Beyond it lie other clusters of galaxies, some of them
